Penketh. Mr. Clarke moved that the Parish Council notify the At the annual meeting of this Council, held on Mon. Administrative Sub-committee of their approval of the day night, there were present—Messrs. Ditchtleld, building of a new school for 500 children, providing the present school was made suitable for accommodat- ing 450. Mr. Warburton seconded, Mr. T. R. Westbrook said if they threw away the accommodation of the present school they would throw away a valuable assest. They had better build a school for 500 now, and increase if they wanted to, later on, and not build a school for 800. Mr. H. J. Westbrook said he was a Member of the sub-committee, and there was a strong feeling that it would be absurd to throw away whatever value they had in the present school. The school would be adopted by the committee if brought up to modern require- ments ; if not it would be discarded and a school for 300 would be built. There was a strong feeling on the sub-committee from the ratepayers' point of view that they ought to consider the managers' pleas. - The resolution was passed. Mr. Bouchier was re-appointed representative manager of the National School. The Chairman complained that for a period of eight months there was not a meeting of the managers. He thought the business ought to be conducted by the whole of the managers and not by one individual. Mr. H. J. Westbrook said if it would strengthen the Chairman's hands he would propose a resolution that they expected the business of the school to be carried out in a busidesselike manner by business men. SHIP CANAL HOARDINGS. It was reported that no reply had been received from the Ship Canal Company to the letter with reference to the removal of the hoardings near the Ship Canal Bridge. The Clerk was instructed ro write to the Company asking for a reply. THE OVERSEERS. Messrs. J. Maley, F. Warburton and F. Pearson were appointed overseers, and Mr. Whitley was heartily thanked for the way in which he had discharged his duties during the past year. THE CLERK'S SALARY INCREASED. It was decided to increase the remuneration of Mr. Bradshaw as assistant overseer and clerk from £66 to Zee per annum. PROPOSED RECREATION GROUND. The Chairman said it had been suggested that a fairly large piece of land should be taken, as near the centre of the village as they could get,it to make a recreation ground. It was also suggested as an alternative that three smaller plots should be taken to serve different parts of the district. For a recreation ground that would serve the whole parish they needed five or six acres. He suggested that a sub-committee be formed to ascertain the price of suitable land for the purpose. If there were any generous-minded landowners about who would give a plot of land it would be a great relief to the parish. - Mr. Brown: And it would perpetuate their memory. The Chairman added that they were all agreed as to the necessity of a recreation ground, mild the sooner they got it the better. Mr. Pearson said the question had been before them more than once, and they ascertained the price of "land which was though to be available. Mr. Egerton- Warburton quoted a price for the field behind the Church Room, and also for a small field below Stockton Quay Canal. He did not remember what it was, and he suggested they should make application again. Mr. Hallows said it would be a heavy burden to have three small grounds and thought it would be cheaper to have one large recreation ground. Mr. H. J. Westbrook said he was not an opponent of a recreation ground. Recreation grounds were all very well if they could get the children into them, but from what he had observed the children preferred to play in the street nearest to their back or front door. What fraction of the child population of Warrington utilized the recreation ground in Warrington Parks? One of the nines the sub-committee ought to inquire about was what the experience of other districts was. There were practical difficulties, and they might havcea recrea- tion grouni which would be in the nature of a white elephant. He did not say it would be. Mr. McDermid suggested that they should have a decent public park and recreation ground. The cost would be spread over sixty years, and while they would not be over-burdening themselves they would be con- ferring a benefit on those who came after them. Mr. Mynekyme said they ought to got as large a piece of ground as possible, on the best terms. He did not think the land behind the Church Room would be large enough. The best site would be down the Hollow. It was nicely situated for a small park. Messrs. Brown, elynekyme, MoDerrnid, H. J. West- brook, and the Chairman were appointed a sub-com- mittee to inquire into the matter and report. Grappenhall. ASSISTANT OVERSEER RESIGNS. The annual meeting of the Grappenhall Parish Council was held in the National School, Grappenhall, on, Tuesday evening, when there were present :—Messrs. J. Charlton Parr, A. Lister-Kaye,Thomas Ireland,Lawrence Belton, F. W. Wareing, William Taylor. S. A. Jenkins William Bacon, Thomas Collins, George Ashton and S. J. Woodcock (clerk). Mr. Wareing proposed the re-election of Mr. Parr as chairman for the ensuina year. Mr. Lister-Kaye seconded, and the motion was carried unanimously. !_ A letter was read from Mr. S. J. Woodcock resiening Ifs position as assistant oversrerand clerk to the Parish i Council, awl on the motion of Mr. Wareing' seconded I by Mr. Lister-Kaye, it was accepted to take effect on. ! the appointment of the new assistant overseer. es O tthemotionof Mr. Wareing, seconded by Mr. s I Jenkins, it was decided that advertisements be inserted ton in thesf local papers asking for candidates for the posi- Jon t assistant overseer of Grappenhall, the applica- o be forwarded to the chairman f the I) • h o e arm Council. Mr. Jenkins moved that the salary be fixed at .£4o.

The first meeting of the newly-elected Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday. There were present :—Rev. T. R. Matthews, Rev. M. Ryan, Mrs. Simpson, Mrs. Wilkinson, Dr. Patterson, Dr. Murray, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, R. Forshaw, J. Billinge, W. Gill, J. Evans, R. Bolton, W. Shinier, W. C. Brooks, M. W. Seton, W. Flannagan, J. Cook, G. Mather J. Backhouse, W. Houghton, A. Brooks, J. C. Sutton (clerk), J. F. Awbery (master), and A Wright (superintendent of Padgate schools). MRS. CLAY'S RETIRENIENT. The CLERK read the following letter from Mrs. Clay :—" I have great pleasure in acknowledging the receipt of your letter of the 4th inst., conveying to me the thanks of the Board for my services as a Guardian for the past twelve years. I should like to take this opportunity of expressing my appreciation of the kind- ness and consideration I have received from every indi- vidual member during that period. For your personal acknowledgment of my services I am especiallygratefal and pleased that such cordial relations have existed during my term of office. I sincerely trust and hope that you will have the best of health to enable you to serve the Board in the very excellent manner which has characterized your work for so many years." THE NEW CHAIRMAN. Mr. BILLINGS, the retiring chairman, extended a cordial welcome to the new Guardians and congratu- lated them upon their election. For the office of chair- man he wished to bring before the Board the same of a real Lancashire gentleman whom it was the wish of the late Guardians—and he felt sure it would be the desire of the newly-elected members—to preside over the deliberations of the Board. He referred to Mr. Silliarn Houghton who had for some time been the senior Guardian and entered upon his twenty-fifth year of service that morning. (Applauee.) During that period he had enjoyed the confidence of the electors of the 'parishes which he had had the honour to represent on that Board and elsewhere. His past work and character -endeared him to all and it would be very fitting, after a quarter of a century's labours in that important department, for -the Board to unanimously confer honour upon one to whom honour was due by electing !Atilt° the chair. (Applause.) Mr. FORSRA.W seconded, and said Mr. Houghton was a genial and able man of business and would fill the chair with honour to the Board and credit to himself. They knew what his work had been at Padgate and in other sections of poor law administration. He had a knowledge of the duties of the chairmanship and they were sure that during his term of office the chair would not lose any of its traditional prestige. Mr. Marraßa supported. Mr. JOLLEY said that after the melifluous oratory of the Cicero of Haydock and the speeches of other country Guardians, it was fitting that one of the town repre- sentatives should tender his tribute to Mr. Houghton's ability. They knew his past work and they anticipated he would have a pleasant and prosperous year of office. They had additional pleasure in seeing Mr. Houghton amongst them that morning in view of the fact that he had recently suffered a severe illness. They hoped be would enjoy good health during the coming year and be able to discharge thehonourable duties of the chairman- ship with the ability and geniality be displayed when he filled the position a few years ago. The CLERK also supported and said he was associated with Mr. Houghton's father in official life sixty years ago. He (Mr. Sutton) served on that Board as a Guardian with Mr. Houghton's eldest brother in the 1860's and with another brother in the 1870's. They had passed away but he trusted that Mr. Houghton as a younger member of a family which had given so much service to the public, would long be with them to help them in the office to which they were about to appoint him.

The resolution was carried with acclamation. Mr. HOUGHTON took the chair and briefly returned thanks. He appreciated the honour very much, although he could not hope to follow modern chairmen with the same ability which they had shown. The Board would, however, have the benefit of his best exertions. His immediate predecessor, Mr. Billinge, had been an ornament to the chair and had occupied the position to the great advantage of the Union in every sense of the word. He would like to impress upon the Board a few words uttered by the chaplain, the Rev. W. Bracecamp, who in his sermon on Mayor's Sunday, told them in all their debates to give credit to each other for good intentions. If that spirit pervaded their meetings it would very much facilitate their discussions.

THE VICE-CHAIRMAN. Mr. BROOKS then proposed the election to the vice- chair of the Rev. T. It. Matthews who, he said, was an old member when he (Mr. Brooks) joined the Board nine years ago. Ho was very unassuming in his work,but thoroughly understood it and when the chairmanship again became vacant they were sure he would make an excellent chairman. Mr. JOLLEY seconded and said Mr. Matthews had shepherded his flock at Earlestown for many years. Though on that Board one or two might at times be a little unruly they hoped he would be able to wield the pastoral staff at Warrington with success. Mr. Flannagan, Mr. Billinge, Mr. Mather, and Mr. Forahaw supported the proposition which was cordially passed.

The Rev. T. R. Marrimws returned thanks and said that if he had not such confidence in the punctuality and regularity of the chairman he would not have accepted the office, because his other duties were very numerous and heavy.

OTHER ELECTIONS. Mr. Jolley was re-elected chairman of the No. 1 (Warrington) Relief Committee and Mr. A. Brooks was re-elected to preside over No. 2 (Country) Committee. A suggestion by Mr. W. C. Brooks that the whole Board should constitute the Relief Committee and arrange a special day for relief business was not con- sidered practicable. the earth for their cause. Your thought must travel into the mysterious star depths to find the forces by which the behaviour of the earth is accounted for. So our Lord teaches that in order to find the explana- tion of the terrible facts of human life we must go out- side the earth to begin with. He introduces us to a tragedy which took place before the world was, or at any rate before man appeared upon the scene. "I beheld Satan as lightning fall from heaven." The state- merit is a brief one but pregnant with meaning. We can complete it without undue use of the imagination by supposing that a spirit of vast intelligence and splen- did place made the evil choice and continued in the evil choice and so fell into permanent ruin. Coming to the earth Our Lord is brought at once into contact with this great arch enemy. " Then was Jesus led up of the Spirit into the wilderness to be tempted of the devil," And any one who knows what life is, and has studied the temptation narrative, must feel the facts are true to life. In is not a mere negation that Our Lord meets but a subtle enemy whose ability is only surpassed by his virulence. fie watches for the weakest moment. presents the very temptation that appeals to a noble mind with the greatest power, backs it with words chosen from the Divine Book itself. Did Jesus Christ, we ask, know what tie was about in this great crisis of His earthly life ? If he did Mr. Campbell is utterly astray. Our Lord not only contended , against the evil one Himself, but He watched his operations in His followers. He was aware of the great temptation that was to beset Simon Peter. Sinion knew nothing of it but the Lord understood. He saw the toils that were being thrown around him and noted how the man's boldness and impulsiveness both marked him out and laid him open to the tempter's malignity. "Simon. Simon," He says, pointing His warning with especial solemnity, " Satan hath desired ' to have thee that he might sift thee as wheat, but 1 have prayed for thee that thy faith fail not." Similarly in regard to Judas Iscariot we are told that Satan entered into Judas with the sop. And is not that the right way to account for such a sin as that of Judas ? These milk and water theories of evil are shown up in their utter weakness and insufficiency when they are brought up against such a sin as that of Judas. Was it a vacuum that betrayed the Son of Man with a kiss? Was it a mere emptiness or defect of something that planned and executed the arch villainy which .has ever since moved the horror of the world? Now what are we to make of the declarations of our Lord himself and these facts so intimately associated with the record of His life? Not long ago araiist the applause of the gallery in a Christian Church Mr. Campbell pushed aside the authority of the Apostle Paul with a wave of his hand. Now he is doing the same by the authority of our Lord Jesus Christ Him- self. But if these solemn and repeated declarations of our Lord mean nothing to this man who stills claims to be a minister of Christ do they mean nothing to us? Are we to suppose that our Lord Himself knew nothing about all this or that He has left behind Him a record of His teaching and His life which on such a matter is utterly unreliable. They talk about their moral consciousness as if that settled everything. Is their moral conscious- ness then superior to the moral consciousness of Jesus

"THE DEVIL A VACUUM." Rev. Sidnay Pitt and tha New Theology. _ _ .

• "The Devil a Vacuum" was the subject of a sermon I on Sunday by the Rev. Sidney Pitt on the New , Theology. He took for his text the words "The Lord said Simon, Simon, Satan lath desired to have thee that he may sift you as wheat. But I have prayed for thee that thy faith fail not." In the course of his sermon Mr. Pitt said: The• apostle of the New Theology is above all things else a master of phrases. We have not to thank him, as far as I know, for much ' light that he has shed on the multitude of topics with which he has undertaken to deal, but we have to thank him at least for this—that he occasion- ally puts his ecsentricities into a chrystaline form which it is impossible either to forget or to misunderstand.' In his last achievement of this description, with the help of a friend, he has sur- passed himself. " The Devil is a vca.uum." Satan has been transformed a good many times. To the meda3val imagination he appeared as a curious zoological speci- men with horns and h6of and tail. In earlier and later times he has often been transformed to an angel of light, but this latest transformation is a masterpiece. He is 'transformed iitto nothing at all. He vanishes iato thin air or vhat is thinner than air, and does not even leave the traditional perfume behind. The pastor of the City Temple seeks him and he is not found. The phrase sounds like a joke, but it is not so. It is meant seriously enough. It condenses as into a nutshell the teaching of the new theology, and of the philosophy that lies behind it on the problem of evil. This light utterance then brings before us the whole dark problem of the existence of moralil in the world. Physical evil, if evil it be, is another matter and may be accounted for on other grounds but moral evil, the evil purpose, the evil will, the evil deed, what are these and why are they here? The answer to these questions lies as we believe in the word free will. Free will involves the possibility of doing wrong. If there •was to be any such thing as real goodness there must be at any rate the possibility of its opposite. Love, honour, self-sacrifice, holiness, and all our nobler characteristics,

they are none of them of any value except apart , from the possibility of the opposite. It is quite a conceiv- able thing that God might have created spiritual beings from whom this possibility was for ever removed. Hen and other spirits might have been so constituted •that they could not possibly go wrong but such beings as this would have been mere machines or mere emanations from another will as, Hr. Campbell apparently thinks they e.re.They would not have been the free intelligences that we are to-day. It is often taught that the actual emergency of evil was necessary to goodness. This I could never see. To begin with at any rate it was never necessary that any man or any spirit should ever make an evil choice but it was possible. The root of sin, as Bishon Gore has recently said, is in the rebellion of the will. Just as a child can rebel against his father and use the very life that sprung from him to fight against him or to oppose his will or to neglbct it, so the spirit that sprung from God is capable of turning against God. I am quite content with the definition of sin as given by Sir Oliver Lodge in his recent Catechism. Sin is the deliberate and wilful act of a free agent who sees the better and chooses the worse and thereby acts iniurionsly to himself and others. Now that was the thing that actually took place. Somewhere in the universe there was a free agent who saw the better and chose the worse. It ought not to have happened but it was not for God to render it impossible by taking away from us our noblest characteristic---the power of choice and self direction. There and then it was that the mischief started and it has gene on and on ever since in an ever multiplying succession of instances in which free beings have setm the better and chosen the worse. So much for the account of evil itself. Then we come to the existence of the evil one, the great prince and head of the powers of darkness. That is a thing we could never have ascertained for ourselves though we might have guessed it. It is a matter of revelation. Our knowledge however is derived not from the fringes of the Bible revelation but from its centre. Our Lord himself has taken' the responsibility of the leading utterances on this great and terrible matter. First he teaches us to regard the origin of evil as extra-terrestrial. There are certain phenomena, the tides for example, which take place upon the earth but which you cannot account for unless you look beyond

t' .4. • 4. , • A 41' S

Christ? Do they know better than He? Nay verily if we are driven to abandon the teaching of our Lord and His Apostles confirmed to us by the Church throughout all ages hitherto, we shall require some better authority for doing so than the nebulous web which the New Theology is spinning from its own entrails. "This is my beloved Son" said the voice of God from the opened heaven over the head of Jesus of Narareth, "hear ye Him." The best Christians in all ages have been content to hear Him and so are we. But what is there after all so unreasonable in the con- ception of a great head and prince of the powers of evil? Even apart from the authoritative teaching of our Lord it seems to us to account for a good many things that are not so easily accounted for in any othet way. Why should it be more difficult to believe in a wicked spirit than in a wicked man? We are obliged to believe in wicked men because we see them and see sadly too much of them. We don't actually see the devil hut we see his works. I remember when I was attending a set of lectures at Oxford a simpering youth telling me that a devil was unthinkable as if the measure of his poor brain was the measure of the possi- bilities of the universe. Such a being was not unthink- able to John Milton. The great English epic is made to turn upon the fate and history of the "Prince and chief of many throned powers who led the embattled seraphim to war." Shakespeare eould form the conception and did. It was a very real thing to him. In one of his noblest passages he compares the fall of Wolsey to the fall of this great spirit "And when he falls he falls like Lucifer

Never to hope again" Perhaps the greatest mind of the last century, certainly the greatest German mind, was that of Goethe and his great poetic masterpiece is made to circle round the malignant activity of Satan in the modern world. nephistophiles is an intel- lectual Satan up to date and absolutely true to the facts of modern life. Burns also has given us some of his deepest thought as well as, his subtlest humour on the same terrible topic. If the devil be ndeed a vacuum then some of the profoundest poetry in he world has llst its meaning. We have to take into account not only the authority of Jesus Christ, not only the thoughts of some at least among our greatest thinkers, but the practical facts of experience as they appear in the phenomena of temptation. Some people , seem to be quite unconscious of this or to escape it, but there are many of us who have found quite enough in our own lives to convince us of a malignant intelligence that works to do us harm. Have you never felt your steps to be dogged by some malignant power waiting and watching for the opportunity to betray you? Have you never found that traps have been laid for you of the subtlest and most seductive kind, and not by any human contrivance either, but by some influence working altogether behind the scenes?

" Principalities and powers. Mustering their unseen array, Wait for thy unguarded hours, Watch and pray ! "

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. RUGBY FOOTBALL.

[By " Ovalist.") There is n' check check on Warrington's progress in the "Cup amapetition so far. On Saturday they reached the final for the fourth time and on all hands , are considered to have a fair chance of `: " the trophy once more. It is reclgaised, however, that in meeting Oldham they will be much more severely tested than they have been as yet and nothing but a display of their .best form can gain them the verdict.

The semi-final at Wigan on Saturday last proved a veritable triumph for Warrington, who trounced Swinton in an altogether unexpected fashion by 21 points tl nothing. This is one of the hollowest victories ever recorded in a Northern Union semi-final tie and even surpasses Warrington's performance in 1901 when they beat Castleford at Broughton by Most people believed that Warrington would •beat the Lions on Saturday, but the• latter set such store by their League victorya few weeks ago that a close con- test was looked forward to. For tem minutes the struggle was really keen and Swinton almost scored, but afterwards they were never "in it." Whether it was the brine baths or not, the Warrington men were in fine fettle and quite ran the blues to a standstill, and the most ardent supporter of the defeated side could not but admit that they were outclassed..

The Swinton forwards were expected to be a big factor in the game bat after the opening quartei they shut up like a concertina. Time after time they were pushed off the ball, especially in the second half; when 13 out of Warrington's 21 points were obtained. It was only in the loose rushes that they made any impression at all, and then they were so frequently off-side as to nullify their efforts. Of the Swinton backs It would be kinder to say nothing. They showed no pretensions to class whatever, and had the ground only been dry and fast they must have had a record score put up against them. As it was the Warring- ton three-quarters did pretty. much as they liked without undua exertion.

Fish was particularly careful to keep out of danger, and one cquld not blame him, seeing what his experi- ences on the Wigan ground have been. He managed to score 2 goals and 2 tries all the same and seemed generally in much better condition for making a great effort if required. There wasbne feature of the match which was remarkable. In the first half Swinton were repeatedly penalised —quite a dozen times I should think. Fish kicked a goal from the first, and then missed every other, though at his four last attempts he hit either posts or cross-bar every time, The ground was wet and the ball heavy or the War- rington skipper might easily have passed his record of 9 goals against Huddersfield in October. I don't think Swinton had a single shot at goal during the afternoon, for if Warrington inftinged it was gener- ally in the Swinton half where they spent most of their time. In fact I never saw a team look less like scoring in the course of a first-class match.

Warrington's success was so pronounced that the score might almost have been doubled had it been necessary. With so many opportunities the backs had a grand time, and considering the condition of the ball they combined very effectively. Hockenhall started moderately, but as the ground dried under a strong wind he found his feet and played brilliant football. Jordan,the other reserve man also acquitted himself well and would have scored twice had he been a bit speedier. The regular hands were all in good form with the exception of Tilley, who had little to do and did that little in an indifferent fashion. I think if special praise is due to anyone it is to Isherwood, who played a fine game throughout. His form is surprising when one recollects that he was a mainstay of the Warrington three-quarter line when the N.U. Cup Competition was inaugurated in 1897, and is still. Thomas was the star of the front division, and well deserved the try he got immediately on resumption, while con- spicuous work was done also by Heath, Harmer and Belton. The" light-weight" was as usual off-side a good deal, but escaped the attention of the referee.

As already announced, the_ final of the Northern Union Cup between Oldham and Warrington will be played on the Broughton Rangers ground on April 27. The Cup Committee of the Union paid a visit to the ground on Monday to make arrangements for the match. The holding capacity of Wheater's Field has never been really tested, but it will accommodate about 30,000 spectators, and an additional stand is being erected. It has been decided to make the admission with an extra charge for the stands. There will be some reserved seats on the big stand at ss. each, and those requiring tickets should communicate with Mr. Platt, 9, Queen-street, Oldham. F. Renton, of Hunslet, has been appointed referee.

When the Cup ,Competition was progressing through the earlier stages thought Oldham were the likeliest team to secure the Cup, but my opinion has been modified considerably by Warrington's return to form. There is no doubt that most of the prophets will tip Oldham to win next Saturday, though in Warrington it is generally held that the success of 1905 will be repeated. It must be remembered that Oldham have had a very severe season and even to-day they will be engaged in the League final with Halifax. All this hard work must tell on the players and tend towards staleness. Warrington have been just com- fortably employed for a month or so and seem to be as fresh as paint. Condition is a very important factor in a strenuous cup-tie and the locals may just have the pull in this respect on the 27th.

Warrington have been fined the maximum penalty £25 for playing the reserve team against Halifax on Good Friday. As this is the second offence the decision is perfectly just, though in view of the fact that the Warrington reserves put ,up a splendid fight and nearly beat Halifax it seems, farcical. If Clubs were allowed to play pranks of this kind with impunity it would be a serious breach of faith with the public. Warrington's only justification will be in winning the Cup next Sitturday.

There has been lunch-discussion of late betwen club representatives of both Lanoashire and Yorkshire with regard to the formation of the League and modifica- tions of the playing rules. An agreement has been come to which should have effect at the general meeting of the Union. Fortunately the changes are not revolutionary, though they will affect a considerable improvement. The new League scheme provides that all Lancashire clubs would play each other, and so will, in like manner, all York- shire clubs. In addition, each Lancashire club would play at least four Yorkshire clubs, and vice versa, with an additional rider that the matches between Lancashire clubs shall be considered a Lancashire League. The scheme was approved, the only club to vote against it being Broughton Rangers, who were in favour of givinr, the present competition a further trial. If this scbe'ine becomes law, the League Com- petition will be a real one and not a make-believe. Clubs like Keighley and. Leigh will not be able to choose their fixtures with a view to securing honours on the cheap, as they did during the past two seasons. The present knock-on rule seems doomed, as it should be. There is altogether too much slovenliness in catching the ball at present.

Warrington are not risking any further penalties by resting their Cap players in the League match with Rochdale Hornets this afternoon. The best team possible has been chosen, only one change being made from last Saturday, and the Hornets seem to be in for

a lively time. the change mentioned is Naylor for Flartner, and the seleceed thirteen are :

_ . Tilley ; fall back, Fish, Isherwood, Taylor and Jordan, threequarters ; Lees and Irockenhall half baelts ; Boardman, Thomas, Heath, Belton, Shugars and Nayispr, forwards. .

The Hornets have selected the f dlowing : k. N. Other; Gibson, Ward, Walker, and A.. J. Richardson; Baxter and Scholfield ; Dearden, . Turner, Stubbs, W. 'Richardson, Brooks, Lilly.

It was hoped that Jenkins and Brooks would have been fit to turn out,but neither is reported fully sound. In fact there is no likelihood at all of the clever half back appearing in the final—news which will be a great disappointment to Warrington's supporters. Brooks has been training hard during the week, and it is expected that his damaged thumb will be quite - right in time for the great game at Broughton.

There is likely to be a great exodus from Warring- ton to Nfanchester next week. Already the Cheshiie Lines Committee have arranged no less than eight special trains. Passengers are strongly advised to book during the week.

Brunner, Mond, and Co., who were the principal promoters, have withdrawn their support, and the undertaking will not be proceeded with. Last year an extension of time was granted by the Board of Trade, and the scheme contemplated two sections of railway—from Warrington to Barnton, and by Great Budworthto Northwich. Hearing screams she rushed into the kitchen and found the deceased lying in front of the fire. A kettle of boiling water, which was on the hob when she left the rooni' I had been upset, and the child was severely scalded! The Examiner, Saturday, Ap

and skirt with only three seams in the two articles. Neither extreme is to be desired, but, taken altogether, the one exploited by the dressmakers is preferable. Many of the newest skirts show fifteen gores, and .the bodices and coatees are veritable works of art—from a patchwork point of view. To the home worker the seven gored skirt presents perhaps the fewest difficulties, being more unlikely to sag and fall out of shape at the hem than the less-gored varieties. About Feather Boas.--In the choioe of a feather boa, the woman who must needs be economical, has often a difficult task before her. In the. first place, colours which may ibasonably be supposed to be more service- able than white, can only be worn with certain costumes, moreover if the colours are light they invariably fade, whilst if they are dark they are very likely to soil the coat or the blouse over which they are donned. White or cream, on the other hand, can be worn with any costume, but then both soil quickly. In these days of inexpensive dry-cleaning however, the latter choice is perhaps the wiser, and provided it is fresh and dainty, few things are far more becoming and smart-looking than a white or cream feather boa. Grey or fawn feather boas are not as a rule a success, and the wise woman will pass them by. _ _

. . The Very Up-to-date.—The extremely fashionable woman with her huge hat, weighted with feathers, and worn at the back of the head, and with her enormous sleeves,that look like ea pes,presents a somewhat comical view, especially from behind. She appears over-loaded and top-heavy, and the effect generally is ludicrous. The woman who would enjoy the reputation of always being well-dressed, should see to it that her head and shoulders are never out of proportion to height, what- ever the prevailing modes may be,a state of affairs fully recognised by Her Majesty the Queen who has never, upon any occasion,worn the huge hat,or balloon sleeves. Only the" divinely tall," and very slender, can venture upon such extremes as modish women are indulging in just now, and even these favoured mortals would do well to exercise a little moderation.

‘The Importance of Ventilation.—lt having been established that influenza is infectious,an Oxford M.D. has written to the "Times "urging the importance of the ventilation of railway carriages. 14 points out that convalescents, and those still ill with influenza, I hurry off as soon as possible to 40rfle mild seaside town, and that, the windows being kept closed, there is very great dancer to the other passengers. It would have been well if the writer had added that ventilation is as beneficial to those who are suffering from influenza as it is to those who are well, but in any event he is no doubt right when he says that a very large proportion of cases of influenza follow a journey by rail, and that there is no surer way of spreading illness than that afforded by closed railway carriages. MADGE.

LANCASHIRF BAND IN FRANCE. 1

Enthusiastic Welcome to Lille

I, The band of the 2nd Battalion the Prince of Wales Volunteers (South Lancashire Regiment) paid a visit to ' Lille on Sunday, and gave a concert in aid of a charity at the Hippodrome. The visit was made the occasion of scenes of enthusiasm such as have never before been witnessed there. The train conveying the visitors from Calais arrived at noon to the thunder of a salute of twenty-one guns, fired by the Artillerymen stationed on the ramparts of the city. Inside the station was the band of the 43rd Infantry Regiment, which played the British National Anthem as the train steamed in. The British Consul, with several French military officers, and the committee of the charity in aid of which the concert was given, sere on the platform, and extended a warm welcome to Lieutenant-Colonel Adam (com- manding the battalion) and the three officers who accompanied him. Lieutenant-Colonel Adam replied in a happy little speech in French, after which a procession was formed and a start made. The scene that met the eyes of the English musicians as they emerged from the station was one of almost indescribable excitement. enormous crowds had gathered along the route, which was guarded by several brigades of gendarmerie, and two companies of Mounted Chasseurs, with their trumpeters were drawn up at the station gates. All the way to the Marie the air resounded with shouts of " Vive l'Angleterre ! " "Vive l'Entente Cordiale!" The Mayor, in extending a welcome to his visitors, said the town was proud of its past glories, but as a friend of peace it delighted in the strengthening of the bonds of sympathy between two great liberal nations. Lieutenant-Colonel Adam having replied, the Mayor presented him with a commemorative medal. A champagne lunch followed, and in the interval before the concert the Englishmen were received by the Prefect Da Nord. The concert commenced at four o'clock, and long before at time the Hippodrome was thronged. Some 4,000 persons were present, and the programme, which extended over two hours, was received with marked cordiality. . . .

A correspondent writing on Monday says :—The concert on Sunday realised £240 for the charity.—The members of the band have been literally loaded with presents. One manufacturer presented eaeh of them with a large box of chocolate, and another sent each a ; lame parcel of cakes, famous locally. A third has given , them three hampers of champagne for the return journey. Otherpresents are pouring in. At the banquet to the officers on Sunday night the company included the Military Governor, the prefect, , the mayor, and the President of the Tribunal. After the banpuet, accompanied by several French officers of rank and by the mayor and the prefect, the British officers visited the principal cafg and drank a glass of the local beer, and this added the finishing touch to their enormous popularity with the Lillois. The adjutant was pressed by each of fourteen wealthy and prominent townsmen, manufacturers or merchants, ' to pay them a visit next morning at their homes and take a glass of wine with them. All offered to send their motor-cars to fetch the adjutant, who is a fluent , French speaker ; and one gentleman, who would take no denial, wanted him to come as early as eight o'clock and "take a bottle of port wine." Captain Goren only saved himself by pleading the imperative orders for his early morning attendance. Monday's proceedings began with a reception of the British officers at the Garrison Club, where all the French officers off duty had assembled to do honour to their guests. Then came a luncheon party at the house of the Consul, Mr. Walker, following which the visiting band played a selection of music in the garden. Then followed a football match between a team from the South Lancashires and a French team—the Iris Club. The match resulted in a victory for the latter by 6 goals to 3. A final concert was held in the Grand Place on Monday evening, and evoked immense enthusiasm. The band left Lille at 11.30 on Tuesday morning. The non-commissioned officers of the garrison escorted their British comrades to the railway station, and large 1 crowds cheered them on the way. Before entering the train the British soldiers, with the consent of their colonel, unfastened the badges of their helmets, their I pouches, and their collars, and distributed them among the French troops as souvenirs of the visit.

PADGATE.

PADGATE CHURCH INSTITUTE.—The annual meeting of the above institute was held in the Church House, the Vicar presiding. The Treasurer and Acting-Secre- tary, Mr. J. C. Davies, read the annual report. He deplored the signs of decreasing interest in the club, and trusted that a strong effort would be made to raise it to its former prosperity. The accounts showed that whereas £4 10s. was brought into the balance-sheet, the year closed with a deficiency of 12s. The report con- gratulated the billiard teams on their success in the Church Clubs' Leaze, the first team winning six matches of the eight played, and gaining the medals for being top of the Lcague. The Criptain, Mr. J. Roberts, won every game he played, and rcored 166 points more than his opponents, The second team won four and lost four matches. Mr. J. C. Davies was elected treasurer. The committee appointed were—Messrs. James Roberts, Charles Roberts, James Monks, P. E. Croft, James Weston; William Weston, T. Smith, J. Smith, H. Smith, J. R. Turner. W. Bowering, and H. Critchley. Messrs. A. Wright and R. Denham were re-elected auditors.

HAYDOCK INVALID'S ACCIDENT.

On Monday, Mr. Brighouse, county coroner, held an inquest at Haydock on the body of John Dierden, aged 65, of Grange-road, Flaydock, who died on the 12th inst. —Ralph Dierden, son of the deceased, stated that on Sunday. the 7th of this month, he left home, leaving his father by the fire in his bedroom, and later he found that he had fallen on to the fender and burned his head.—Dr. Thompson stated that for several years the deceased had suffered from chronic bronchitis, and two years ago he had an epileptic stroke. Since then he had been very feeble. There was no evidence that the accident had accelerated death.—A verdict of "Accidental death" was returned.

Large numbers of presents addressed to the "Heir Apparent" of Spain, sent by French ladies, especially in the south, have been returned to the senders, as the i authorities, n accordance with the usual custom, are refusing all presents.

Crippled from Paralysis. A Missionary's Advice

Mrs. H. Wilkinson, of 44, Flax-road, Belgrave, Leicester, writes : "My little girl, aged 3, suffered from Infantile Paralysis and Nerve and Muscular Weakness. We had her some time under medical treatment, but derived no benefit. She could not walk ; alone, or even stand, or turn over in bed. At last I was recommended by Mr. Abbott, Police Court Missionary in this town, whom you had cured of a bad case of Paralysis of the i nerves, to try Dr. Cassell's Tablets. I did so, and after five " months' treatment my little girl could walk by herself, and she iS much stronger and three pounds heavier. DRESS-FASHION-HOME,

SUMMER HALF MOURNING.

So many people who have to wear mourning in the heat of the summer are puzzled 'what to choose for cool morning wear. Of course, in the afternoon there are all sorts Of lovely black transparencies, but a completely black washing frock is a difficulty indeed, says a Veen writer. If it is permissible to include a tiny white pin spot, the old-dashioned bird's-eye "cotton" in black is a useful choice worn with embroidered muslin collar and cuffs, or a little chalk-white Irish guipure at neck and elbows. Made in the simplest blouse-suit style this is an infaluable frock, and another with a hair-line of white on black may accompany it. Black linen, of course, is always poSsiMe, and there is shantung, and a material which looks like a combination of the two, for all black gowns. Black muslin, either plain, or again with a pin-spot, embroidered in white or mauve, is always nice, and a grey muslin dotted with black and trimmed with black lace—never combine any muslin but pure white with guipure or bands of insertion is my advice— can be made most becoming. to women still quite young as well as to their elders.

===== 0002601_19070420_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
SKIRT WITH SEPARATE FOUNDATION.

Quite of the latest and most approved type is the graceful skirt pictured in our illustration. Fine cloth, silk, or voile would all be suitable materials for its realisation; but the lining must, in any case, be separate from the overskirt except for the joining at the waist. The skirt is made with a shaped seam up the centre of the front, skid has just a suspicion of gathers at

the waist. . Cut to rest upon the ground all round, the skirt has the merest suggestion of a train at the back, and is suitable as an accom- paninient to dressy muslin and silk blouses, or to wear with one of the new and extremely smart short sack coats of silk. It should prove a valu- able addition to the summer wardrobe, especi- ally as it requires but little making. The two belts sketched in the picture are else of the latest type, the one on the left being composed of black satin, the other of fine soft doeskin.

A SOCIETY OF SOCIETY GIRLS.

Lady Muriel Gore-Browne ii lending her house for a meeting of the girls' branch of the Nati- onal Society of Day Nurseries, and Lady Bea- trice Moore has consented to be president for the first year. This branch, says the Onlookcr, is to consist of young society girls, who will have their own committee, secretary, and treasurer, and their aim is to raise funds for the society and to hold a ball once a year in aid of the same charity.

===== 0002601_19070420_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
TEli MIIBREOOI4 HAT.

The mushroom hat as worn on the Riviera this year by Frenchwomen shadows none of the charms of brow or coiffure, says a Skath writcr. It is set on the back of the head and shades the back of the heck like a Sailor's, sou'-wester. The pretty waved coiffure and the face are unshaded save by a parasol.. Englishwomen adopt this way of wearing the mushroomhat less whole- heartedly—or perhaps I should say less whole- headedly; they wear the hair high and the hat poised slightly back on it. In this way they gain the double advantage of some little shade for the eyes, aid have nothing to -obscure the setting of the head and its carnage—usually a good point of. the average Englishwoman.

===== 0002601_19070420_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
PRETTY SHIRT BLOUSE.

Altogether smart and pleasing is the becoming little shirt blouse pictured in our sketch. As the idea is that it should form an accompani- ment to a dressy afternoon. skirt or costume,

some rather good material should be chosen for its realisation—silk, linen, taffetas, soft satin, and foulard all being suitable for the purpose. Down the centre front runs a stitched plastron of the material, which is cut out in a deep point at the top and fastens over with a single row of buttons. On either side of thi, plastron come four or five short tucks, set into a stitched shoul- der strap, which comes well down on to the sleeve to give the all-important long-shoulder

effect. From the neck ;urns back a small stitched collar, the vest and neckband being made of spotted net finished with a. cravat of xelvet Zhe kiuue ides. ausrht

A NEW WALE,

It is curious, remarks the Tager, how each Fear women seem to adopt a different walk; either in some subtle way our clothes influence our walk or it is that when everybody begins to walk with a smart swing we get bored with the sight and promptly droop and dawdle. It was most marke /1 how with the advent of tiny one- sided hats :Ind short, swinging skirts we all be- came quite aggressively perky and apparently always in a hurry; and now under the influence of poetic hats and long skirts we have utterly collapsed. The long Empire or Grecian draperies and Japanese shoulders that are influencing us just, now cause quite a new walk. We droop ex- cessively and gently sail along, our long, clinging tkirts making short, slow steps a positive necessity.

TO-DAY'S REOIPE.

PIGION PlE.—Clean and wipe four pigeons. Split and put in a saucepan with enough good broth to nearly cover. Simmer slowly until ten- der. Take out and remove some of the larger bones. Cut the hearts and livers into bits. Have ready four hard-boiled eggs, sliced. Butter a deep pudding dish qnd fill with layers of the pigeons' eggs and minced heart and liver. Sea- son with salt and pepper, make a gravy with a tablespoonful each of butter and flour, and one and a-half cupfuls of broth. Season with salt and pepper, and pour one-half into dish. Cover with a layer of paste, making an incision in the middle of the crust, and ornamenting the edge with braids. leaves, or rolls of crust. Bake for one hour in a rnoneratery not oven. w Lea nearly done, brush over the top of the pie with the yolk of an egg, beaten with a little milk. Gem hot with the rest of the gravy.

HOUSEBREAKING AT WARRINGTON.

Important Capture by the Police.

At the Warrington Police Court on Tuesday, before Mr. C. Jackson Holmes and Mr. W. Richardson, Thomas Bell, 128, Berry's-lane, St. Helens, and Hugh Beattie, last from Lymm and formerly of Knutsford-road, War- were charged: (1) Breaking and entering the dwelling- house of Edward Powell, 13, Edgeworth-street, between 3 p.m. and 9 p.m. on Wednesday, December 26th, and stealing articles of a total value of £lO, the property of Edward Powell ; (2) breaking and entering the house of William Wood, joiner, 6, Orford-avenue, between 2.30 p.m. and 6 p.m. on Thursday, February 21st, and stealing goods to the value of £l5, the property of Isabella Wood; (3) breaking and entering the dwelling- house of Annie Clayton, widow, 280, Manchester-road, Warrington, between 2.50 p.m. and 3.30 p.m. on Tuesday March 12th, and stealing jewellery valued at £l2, the property of Nellie Clayton. Superintendent Monaghan, detailed the facts of the cases. After the thefts had been reported to the police nothing further was heard until Tuesday last week when Bell went to a pawnbroker's shop in St. Helens, and offered a watch taken from Mr. Powell's house in pledge. The pawnbroker had information from the Warrington police about the robbery, and he at once sent for the police, and Bell was arrested. He was brought to Warrington and in a statement incriminated Beattie,who was arrested. Prisoners were charged together, and Beattie said, "I did that job. Let him (Bell) go." He (the superintendent) now wished to withdraw the case against Bell, and call him as a witness. Bell did not know that the watch be tried to pledge had been stolen. —On Friday, he (the Superintendent) bad an interview with Beattie, and told him there was a lot of jewellery missing. Beattie said," Well, if you will take me out I will show you where it is." He declined to say where , it was without going out, and they took a cab and went to a house in Knutsford-road. In the kitchen Beattie pat his foot on a flag and said," It is there. Lift that flag up." Buried beneath the flag they discovered a canister containing stolen property, relating to other robberies in Orford-avenue and Manchester-road, and also the proceeds of two robberies at Stockton Heath, one at Runcorn. one at St. Helens. and one at Widnes.

Evidence was given by the owners of the recovered property. _

. Bell iave evidence to the effect that Beattie made him a present of the watch. He was not aware that it had been stolen.

Prisoner had nothing to say in answer to the charge. Superintendent Monaghan remarked that there were three other cases of housebreaking which prisoner admitted. These were at Miss Coxon's, Latchford •, at Mr. Donaldson's, 38, Crosfield-street, from which place some Australian mining share certificates and other important documents were stolen; and at another house in Manchester-road, which was entered on the day of General Buller's visit.

Prisoner was committed to the Liverpool Quarter Sessions for trial.

FATHER RECOMMENDS CUTICURA TO EVERY ONE

"When my little boy was about four years old (he is now about six and a half years) a spot appeared on the side of his face just under the ear. After a time it broke and spread further down. I took him to a doctor as soon as it appeared. He was under his orders for about three or four months when he was much worse. Seeing in a paper a Cuticura advertisement I went and got a complete set of the Cuticura Remedies, the Cuticura Ointment, Cuti- cura Soap, and Cuticura Resolvent. I took him away from the doctors and gave him these Cuticura Remedies. I used two large bottles of Resolvent, five tablets of Cuticura Soap, and two or three tins of Cuticura Ointment. From the first bottle he began to mend and before he had finished the two he was wholly mended. About twelve months after he broke out again at the back of the neck and it spread all over the head. I had one large and two small bottles of Cuticura Resolvent, three tins of Cuticura Ointment, and four tablets of Cuticura Soap before I got it mended. I think it took me about three months each time. It is now about eighteen months since he was ill, and it has not broken out again, so I think he is all right now, thanks to Cuticura. I always recommend Cuticura wherever I can, and I will always have pleasure in answering any Inquiries. R. Scarth, 25 Newport Rd., Middlesborough, York, June 24, 1906."

London has contrived to THE COLONIAL express the national feeling PREMIERE. concerning the Imperial

Conference. It exhibited an overwhelming gladness at the sight of the representatives of our Colonies. This demonstra- tion of the pride we take in our kin beyond the seas will we venture to anticipate, be the chief result of such a gathering of the leading statesmen living under the British flag as has not pre- viously been seen. The practical outcome of the Conference will be to make the Colonies better known to the Mother Country and the Mother Country more appreciative of the Colonies. The idea that some new tie is to be forged has no foundation in the facts of the case, or, indeed, in ' common sense. No new tie is necessary. It is better—very much better—that the Colonies should be attached to us by silken bonds than by steel chains, as Sir Wrurrun N LAunran, with his happy eloquence, said at the Guildhall. There is no loyalty like the loyalty of free men, and no bond like the bond of freedom. We welcome the Conference, for one thing, because 1 from the beginning it demonstrate" the vanishing nature of Ike situation mated by Mr. thitAxmlip.

LAIN. Sir WILFRID LAURIER'S speech was throughout the best kind of evidence possible that the teaching of Mr. LYTTELTON'S predecessor at the Colonial Office has in no way sunk into the hearts of the wiser and abler of our Colonial states- men. On the one hand, Mr. CHAMBERLAIN incited the Colonies to exhibitions of disloyalty if we at home declined to return to a Protectionist system by way of giving Preference to Colonial products. On the other hand, we were assured that our Colonies would fall from us if we did not accept an " offer " which had, in fact, never been made The answer to this is that the Colonies do not demand Preference as a condition of loyalty. Their faithfulness to the Mother Country is in no way whatever dependent on a bribe. "The Confer- ence," said Sir WILFRID LAURIER, "stands to witness before all the world that the British Empire is_a living entity." The Colonial representatives he went on to say, come without gins, protesta- tions, or protests. They are the mouthpieces of ,no grievances. They ask for nothing and they joffer us nothing. The Chamberlain bubble has burst.

Nothing could be more remarkable than the manner in which General BOTHA has been received in this country. He is the great figure of the Conference. In contrast to the insanities and the inanities of Mafeking night, there now comes out in bold relief, the national admiration of a bold and daring foe. General BOTHA is the most popular and the most admired man in England at the moment. This is something really wonderful to think of. A more splendid justification of Liberal policy there could not be. The establishment of a Constitu- tion has won the heart of the Transvaal, and the choice of a Boer Premier with a record like than of General Botha has won the admiration of England. Sir WILFRID LAURIER again spoke the appropriate word when he said, "Only one nation in the world would have dared to dn what has actually beer done, and that nation is England." We have made a new, loyal Colony because we have racted on the principle that " There is no bond like the bond of freedom."

Mr. Asquith's Budget is the THE BUDGET. work of a statesman, a financier, and a man of courage. It is pre-eminently a sound, fair, and moderate Budget, with the strengthening of the national credit as its immediate aim, the reform of direct taxation as its most tangible achievement and the paving of the way for soccial reform as its ultimate underlying purpose. The Chancellor has resisted the temptation—if he felt it at all—to devote some portion of Lis surplus to the remission of the war taxes on such popular lines as the phrase " a free breakfast table" suggests. Yet the Budget will be popu'ar after all. It presents a solid front to the critics, eases the burden of the humbler income tax-payer, and gives so safe a guarantee of Old Age Pensions in the near future that it is difficult to civil at. The extreme Labour men are far from satisfied with the postpone ment for another twelve months of • the Old Age Pension Scheme, but they have really little ground for complaint. If it had been possible and consistent with a coherent financial policy, we have no doubt at all that Mr. Asquith would have boldly introduced Old Age Pensions this year. He has instead made a very important provision for the near future in earmarking two millions and a quarter as the nucleus of a fund for the relief of necessitous old age next year. The long-delayed pensions are more than promised —they are practically in sight—and not only the middle-classes, but the workers themselves, have solid reason for satisfaction in the new Budget, which thus happily unites sound finance and social reform.

Lord Tweedmouth's state- BRITISH NAVAL meet 88 to the attitude of POLICY. the Government on the maintenance of British naval supremacy ought to remove all justification from those who have been anxious to represent the Premier and his followers as desirous of weakening the strength of the first line of defence. Great Britain, his Lordship declared, will go into the Hague Conference absolutely unpledged to reduction unless other nations agree to a diminution of armaments. There is, of course, nothing new in that standpoint. No British Government with any regard to its OWE stability in Parliament or the country could do more. Benevolent intentions are all very well, but the history of the world unfortunately has not left us with very deep faith in the good intentions of our neighbours, and while we breathe love and confidence it is always well to have the bulldog at hand. As to the two-Power standard, that is an axiom which few people will now dispute and the Government is determined to maintain it. If other countries are willing to grasp the olive branch we hold out well and good. They have a fair offer before them. The country which is roost power- ful is willing to put a limit on bloated armaments if they will agree to do likewise. But, so long as they persist in building great fleets, so long, ship for ship, shall we lay down the vessels necessary to enable us to defend ourselves against any two of them in case of attack. The country, according to those responsible for naval policy, has never had a sea service so efficient and ready for war as is the Navy at the present time. That is a statement which must evoke universal satisfaction. We do not seek war. Our efforts have been and will be constantly devoted to the maintenance of peace. But should war be forced upon us we shall be at home. That is the message the First Lord of the Admiralty has voiced.

Mr. Tree has been cateking MR. TREE IN HOT it, and no wonder. The WATER. Germans prefer the intel- lectual way. Mr. Tree's method is the spectacular, and therein he follows the later Irving traditions. But it seems to be what London requires at nine o'clock in the even- ing, when it has dined. The drama of Shakespeare must take place in the heads of his hearers. It is not a thing to be seen from the eyes outwards It does not exist even until it has begun to purge the passions through pity and terror. Coleridge studied Shakespeare for a long term of years, and in that constant association became increasingly satisfied of the poet's profundity and inexhaustibleness. But his study had nothing to do with limelight and tableaux. A shrewd busi- ness man once declared that he read Shakespeare because it enabled one to know people and under- stand them, and added: "I owe my success in life to Shakespeare." This was not the Shake- speare of His Majesty's Theatre. Yet, as Zola said: "While remaining real par excel- lence Shakespeare did not reject the ideal.' There are three things which must always dispute it with the presentations of material luxury and conventional elocution— the facts that Snake- spear° belongs to the great psychological dramatist; that he gave the world perhaps the greatest "human " revelation ever vouchsafed it, whereby he, who already had "too copious a flow of eloquence "(vide Ben Jonson), is merely hampered by the adventitious in delivering his message; and the further fact that he is of the first of poets, and you cannot gild the fine gold. Mr. Tree's Berlin visit will have done good if it only serve to show us what Englishmen are missing by neglecting the serious reading of Shakespeare.

" A lie is an abomination in the sight of the Lord, out a very present help in time of trouble." Already about a dozen applications have been received for the post of Chief Constable of Warrington.

* * *

The tendency of modern conversation (writes a " Manchester Guardian" correspondent) is all in the direction of exaggeration, and I give the following as an example. On returning from business yesterday I had occasion to reprove my daughter for some neglected task, when she replied with every appear- ance of sincerity: "Oh, father, I really had not time to do it. In the afternoon a million people called, and stayed for ever !" On inquiry I discovered that two old ladies had paid a visit and remained for exactly thirty-five minutes. * *

The new bowling green at A.rpley Station for the use of local London and North-Western employees will make a splendid adjunct to the Institute at Bank Quay. Not only so, but it is an acquisition to the town as well and a bright spot in an otherwise drab district. Local railway workers are being capitally catered for in Warrington, and yesterday also saw the opening of a miniature rifle range at Bank Quay This has been most ingeniously constructed out • three passenger coaches built end to end into one another and makes a really model little range. How much credit for all these recent improvements is due to Mr. I. T.Williams we do not know, but we strongly suspect that his energy, tact, and thoughtfulness have been largely responsible for bettering the lot of the hard-working London and North-Western staff in Warrington.

L. Drn.

Northern League and West Lancashire Cup Ties 5 Friendlies

Ap , p, ptiy. Death of Mr. S. C. Rathbone. We regret to record the death at the early age of 22 years of Mr. Stanley C. Rathbone, son of Mr. Andrew Rathbone of the Mount, Grappenhall, which sad event took place on Tuesday after a long and painful illness. Nearly a year ago the deceased was compelled owing to ill-health to relinquish his position in the Borough Treasurer's Office, Warrington much to the regret of his colleagues to whom he had endeared himself by his kindly disposition. This early death has brought to a close a most promising career, for Mr. Rathbone gained prominence by the diligent performance of his duties, together with his successes in accountancy examinations and at our Local Technical Institute. It is only two years since he obtained the distinction of of being placed first in all Lancashire and Cheshire for advanced book-keeping, and carried off the special prize granted by the Union of Lancashire and Cheshire Institutes. I The interment took place at the Cemetery on Thursday in the presence of a large number of relatives and friends. The funeral service was conducted by the Rev. Chas. Harvey Cook. The chief mourners were Mr. Andrew Rathbone (father), Messrs. Percy, Harry, Frank, Jackson, Hornby, and Edward Rathbone (brothers), Messrs. A. H. Rathbone, George Walling- ton, and S. Taylor (uncles), Messrs. T. Critchley, T. Wallington and R. Allen 11(cousins), together with Messrs. James Kenworthy, G. J. Brown, Henry Ley- land and F. Eyre (friends). The Borough Treasurer's, Town Clerk's, and Education departments of the Corporation were represented. Numerous floral ributes were received from relatives and friends.

There were no goals from marks. RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The first meeting for the year of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton on Tuesday when there were present :—Mesers. A. Green- way, J. Bolshaw, E. Britch, P. Mounfield, G. Egerton- Warburton, J. Stokes, J. Whitlow, A. Broome,T. Baxter, P. Booth, C. E. Linaker, J. H. Davies, T. Wright, H. Mawdsley, S. Woodward, W. Harrison, G. Pilling, S. Horton, J. L. Jones, E. Bretherton, C. Horton, C. F. Bell, J. Spruce, W. M. Morris, P. Collins. T. Wood, J. Douglas, A. R. Norman, J. Renshaw, 3. Hallows, J. Mynekyme, W. Winstanley, W. Weir, J. B. Chadwick, A Haining, T. Antwis, L. Bolton. G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (assistant clerk), J. Pearson, T. Cook, and W. R. dePrey (relieving officers). .

ELECTION OF CIiAIRMA.N. Mr. Linaker moved the election of Mr. Baxter to the chair, in place of Mr. Norman. It was well-known that at that Board the vice-chairman in time became the chairman, and Mr. Norman had occupied the chair for the recognized number of years. Mr. Baxter was a very old member of the Board and had given a good deal of time and energy to the furtherance of the busi- ness. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Norman seconded. He never expected to be again elected to the chair. He believed in the honours going round and he thought the selection of Mr. Baxter was a very wise one. The proposition was unanimously carried. Mr. Baxter thanked the members. He hoped he would be able to conduct the business of the Board in as friendly and courteous manner as had always been the case in the past. Mr. Spruce, on the motion of Mr. Linaker, seconded by %Ir. Stokes, was appointed vice-chairman of th 3 Board.

Cricket. '

At last there is a Warrington and District Cricket League. This is a feature which has long been wanted and doubtless will serve the purpose of re-awakening interest in Warrington in the summer sport. The League makes a good start with a membership of eight clubs and as the president, Mr. James Dodds, is presenting handsome gold medals to the winners, a keen competi- tion is anticipated. The competition is to be conducted on the principle of counting two points for a win and one for a draw, and the play commences on Saturday next when Bewsey Wesleyans entertain Walker's Brewer

On Thursday last before Messrs. Robert Stone (chairman), F. J. Wood, and Roger C. Parr. AN UNDESIRABLE ALIEN.—Mr. Cripps, representing the Liverpool Corporation, asked the Bench to make an order to send Rachel Gordor back to Russia. She had come from Russia and gone to the United States, but was sent back to England as a person of unsound mind and an undesirable alien. She was taken to the Liver- pool Workhouse and afterwards to Winwick Asylum.— Order made. _ _ _

FOOTBALL IN THE STREET.—John Fitzpatrick,Sarauel Allen, George Wakefield, Cunliffe Edgar, William Brodie and Robert Mason—youths aged from 14 to 17— were summoned for playing football on the highway in Wellington-street, Earlestown. All except Edgar were fined 2s. 6d., and Edgar is., he being the youngest. CRUELTY TO HORSES.—James Fletcher was summoned for cruelly ill-treating a pony on Good Friday, at New- ton.—Abraham Dutton, of Earlestown, and Mrs. Peak, proved that the defendant was violently thrashing the pony, which was about 14 hands high, and was harnessed to a cart in which were the defendant and four others. They had been to a football match at Earlestown, and were driving back to Ashton, and this occurred in Ashton-lane.—P.S. Bannister and P.C. Hughes, who were told of it and saw the pony afterwards, spoke to weals and bruises showing the beating it had received, and it seemed exhausted.—Defendant and one of the men in the cart with him denied the cruelty, saying the pony would not go, and got one leg over the shaft.—The Chairman thought the pony, in refusing to go with such a load, showed more sense than the driver.—Thew Bench thanked the witnesses who had helped the police, and fined defendant 10s. and costs.—Wm. Groom, for driving a horse in an unfit condition, at Haydock, was ordered to pay code. AN OBJECTIONABLE NEIGHBOUR.—Sarah Garrity was summoned for assaulting Elizabeth Grundy at Wargraxe-road, Earlestown.—Mr. Knowles, on behalf of the .plaintiff, said the case would be shortened because the defendant had pleaded guilty. There had been disputes between the parties through annoyance caused by the defendant, and the latter had used dis- gusting language imputing very bad things to the plaintiff. He was rather sorry she had pleaded guilty, because be would otherwise have called several respect- able witnesses to prove the respectability of the plaintiff. He asked the Bench to bind defendant over to keep the peace.—The Bench did so, and also bound her husband over in £5 for her good behaviour. DRUNKENNESS, &C.—The following were fined for drunkenness—William Downs,Edward Mullahy,Absalom Harter,Patrick Naughton (Golborne),and Price Roberts, 2s. 6d. each and costs; William White, John Hinds, ILizetta Harter, William Rigby, Jonathan °leave and Anthony Chesworth (Haydock), ss. each and costs. In the case of Mary Murray and her son, Thomas Murray, of Hand and Banner Yard, Newton, both of whom were summoned for drunkenness and disorderly conduct, the woman was fined 2s. 6d. and costs, while her son got the benefit of the doubt and was dismissed.—William Hill was fined 10s. and costs, and Lena Brown ss. and costs, both for using foul language at Earlestown.

Index to Inside Pages. 2, Board of Guardians. Parish Council Meetings. District News. 3. Women's Chat and Fashions. Housebreaker Captured. 0. Dr. Beet's Visit. Presentation to Mr. and Mrs. Rose. Football. 7. Science Notes. Mr. Robert _Plerpoint.

The late Member for Warrington, Mr. Robert Pierpoint has this week undergone a second operation at his London residence. We are sure our readers will be glad to learn that the operation was entirely successful, and that it is expected Mr. Pierpoint will soon be restored to health. Shocking Accident. - - -

A terrible accident occurred at Messrs. Garnett's works on Thursday. Shortly after one o'clock Terrence Rogers, a single man, lodging in Dixon- street, was talking to a fellow workman who was stood in the lift. Rogers had his head over the guard gate when, without warning, the lift descended and the unfortunate man's head was trapped. He was shockingly injured and was removed to the Infirmary, where he lies in a critical condition. Band of Hope Festival. _ -

The event of the year in local Band of Hope circles —the annual festival—is announced to take place next week. In the past the committee have been satisfied with one production but this year they are more ambitious and it is expected that crowded audi- ences will assemble at the Parr Hall on Wednesday and Thursday evenings. The programme includes many attractive items, the old features having been retained whilst welcome new departures are made. On Wednesday the Mayor will preside, whilst on Thursday Mrs. James Dodds will occupy the chair. Further particulars will be found in our advertising columns.

Accident to Mr. R. 11 Posnett. On Wednesday evening Mr. R. H. Posnett of Run- corn (brother-in-law of Mrs. Charles E. Parker) was the victim of a motor accident which might have been attended with very serious consequences. Mr. Posnett was returning to Runcorn from Penketh at 6.45 when the car collided with a telegraph pole at the corner of the °nerdley-road and Mr. Posnett and his chauffeur were thrown out. Both were badly bruised, and Mr. Posnett returned to Penketh Lodge where he was detained by Mr. Parker as an "in-patient." We are pleased to state that he has now recovered sufficiently to be able to return home.

Twenty Miles a Day or a Kicking.

The ;vanderings of a fright, intelligent:- boy, William O'Nally (11), were related to the Warrington magis- trates, on Thursday.—The boy said his mother made him tramp twenty miles a day, or kicked him. He walked from Warrington to Manchester, Oldham, Rochdale, Halifax, and Keighley, through other town to Doncaster, Barrow, Ulverston, Coniston, Millom, Whitehaven, Workington and Maryport, and back to Warrington.—On returning to Warrington the boy was in a deplorable condition, with - rags pinned round his body, and he was terribly hungry.—His mother was sentenced to a month for neglecting him, and the boy is to be adopted by the Guardians. The Chairmair said the lad would make his mark if looked after. Education Committee.

The Education Committee will meet at the Town ' Hall at 6 o'clock on Monday evening. Mr. A. J. Jolley has been appointed Chairman of the Finance Committee; Councillor Luke Ellison, Chairman of the School Attendance Committee; and Alderman Bolton, Chairman of the Sites and Buildings Com- mittee; and Canon Stevenson, Chairman of the School Management Committee. Among the matters on the minutes for consideration are: The sanction of the Local Government Board to the raising of loans amounting to £9,569 and .t 637 for the erection and furnishing, respectively, of the Bolton Council school, the former amount to be repayable in 30 years and the latter in 10 years ; the raising of the maximum salary of assistant schooll attendance inspectors from 355. to 40s. per week. There was a large gathering of the congregation of Christ Church, Latchford, in the schools, Wash-lane, on Thursday evening when the esteemed Vicar of the parish, the Rev. G. C. Briggs, (whose marriage to Miss Fairclough, daughter of Mr. John Fairclough, the Grange, Latchford, takes place at Christ Church on Tuesday), was the recipient of several handsome presents. Mr. LT. Banks (superintendent) presented him with a barometer subscribed for by the Sunday School teachers. _ _ MAINTENANCE OF EPELEPTICS. The Clerk presented a copy of a petition forwarded by the Carlisle Union, in which Parliament was asked to make a grant from imperial funds of 4s. a week in respect to each inmate and lunatic maintained in a Workhouse.

Mr. A. R. Norman moved that the petition be not supported. It would be tantamount to an admission of their liability to maintain epeleptics whereas they had always contended that these cases should be treated in an asylum. Mr. Woodward seconded and the motion was approved.

DELIGHTFUL DISHES.

Many delightful dishes can be made with currants,and since the King's physician has spoken so enthusiastically of this fruit, one has a secure and comfortable feeling about it, knowing that the opinion of so eminent an authority must be correct. This great doctor's faith in that old-fashioned delicacy—Currant Bread—is so strong that he would have people of all ages and classes account it a necessary article of daily food.

CONGREGATIONAL SUNDAY SCHOOL.

The 66th anniversary of the Congregational Sunday School was held on Sunday, when special services were held morning and evening, the preacher being the Rev. T. G. Taylor, of Kendal. A musical service was rendered in the afternoon by children and the choir, assisted by Miss Turner, Miss J. Howard, Miss Eckersley, Miss M. Williams, Miss Elsie Owen, and Mr. J. Sephton. Mr. D. R. Owen presided at the organ. OCCASIONAL COURT.

At the Occasional Court, on Monday, before Mr. T. Ormand, Edward Taylor was charged with being drunk and disorderly, and was fined 10s. and costs.—John Walton, of Earlestown, and Mary Walton his mother, of Warrington were charged with being drunk in Houghton-street, on Sunday night, and were each fined 2s. 6d. and costs.—John Flaherty, a notorious character, was charged with being drunk and disorderly. He hsd many previous convictions against him, and was fined 10s. and costs.

ST. HELENS VOLUNTEER ADJUTANCY.

Capt. Oakeley, the adjutant of the 2nd V.A. §auth LancaShire Regiment, has retired from the Army after a service of twenty years, His place as adjutant of the St. Helens Volunteer Battalion has been filled by the appointment of Capt. J.D.E. Travis-Cook, of the line battalion. Capt. Travis-Cook has for some time been. connected as lieutenant with the depot at Warrington- the new adjutant is a colonial, who was formerly cons nected with the Natal Mounted Police. He won hi- commission by gallant conduct in the siege of Lady smith, and in the subsequent fighting he earned much distinction, and was later attached to the South Lanca- shire Regiment. ST. HELENS LADY'S DEATH.

The greatest sympthy was expressed in St. Helens, on Saturday, when it became known that Mrs. Knowles. wife of Dr. F. J. Knowles, of Hardshaw-street, had died early that morning at Colwyn Bay. Mrs. Knowles had been ill aboat three months. Her father, Mr. R. G. Brooke, has only just recovered from a most serious accident, and the untimely death of Mrs. Knowles has caused much sorrow to all her friends.
