SOCIALIST DEMONSTRATION. A GODLESS AND UNHOLY TOWN.

• The St. Helens Socialist Society held a-deniOn-stration on Sunday at the Hippodrome. Mr. J. H. Standring J.P., presided over a large gathering, and said Socialism was the cause of humanity. They were there to repeat their protest against the existence of unnecessary suffering. They saw the wonderful progress of mankind and the wealth of the world and their own nation, and yet they were content to see the poverty and misery of the great mass of the people. He , saw no other solution for the problem of unem- ployment and poverty than Socialism, with its , desire for a- change in our commercial system. , —Mr. Frank Johnson moved a resolution declaring ' belief in the claim of Socialism that poverty and unem- pinyment need net exist, and could be done away with only by the public ownership of the means of life, so that land and machinery should yield to labour a full fife for every man and woman.—lr. Dan Irving, of Burnley, supported, and said they would speedily have Socialism ruling the country, because it would commend itself to the commonsense and judgment of the people. Pre- vious speakers had talked about unempleyment, but in St. Helens the evils arising from employment were a thousand times worse than any unemployment they could have. It was difficult to realise that people sacri- ficed themselves by working in the chemical works and before the glass furnaces of St. Helens and in other employments which eat up life with a ruthlessness worse than any other form of employment. His wrath fell not so much upon the capitalists and the landlords as upon the people who were willing to allow them- selves to be used in these ways for so little recompense. The great barrier to progress was the disinterestedness and the ignorance and the apathy of the great mass of the men and women. He urged the tremendous import- ance to the people of a proper regard for the life of the child. It was high time that the people shook off their apathy, and by means of Socialism and intellectual effort secured control of the methods of production and of the organisation of industry, and directed the wealth of the country for the benefit of the people.— Mr. George Lansbury, of the Poplar Board of Guardians, said that, coming from London, he wondered what and who the saint was that St. Helens was named after, for a more ungodly, unholy place he had never set eyes dn. (Applause.) They cheered that statement; but were they content to live in it? If the workers of St. Helens were only determined that,it should not be, it need not be. They had acts of Parliament about the smoke nuisance, but their wives and children lived under a pall of smoke every day. There it hung over town ; and how the sun ever got through the cloud of smoke he did not know. Still the workers sat down and submitted to it. Walk through the town, and they saw nothing but ugliness, ugliness, ugliness; no redeem- ing feature about it, but one dead level of ugliness and misery. Did they as working men ever think of the kind of life that they doomed their wives and children to lire. This was all caused by the unequal distribution of the wealth of the land. That state of things could only exist when the working classes fought for each other. He appealed to all present to throw in their lot with the Socialists of the town, who were working for liberty and freedom and the right to live in the true spirit of brotherhood and true humanity. The workers of the world had no enemies but capitalists and in the struggle th 3 people had nothing to lose, but they had a world to gain. The resolution was carried unanimously, and votes of thanks closed the pro- ceedings.

BANK AMALGAMATION. THE UNION BANK OF MANCHESTER, LIMITED, AND DOWNES & CO., NANTWICH„

The Union Bank of Mano"'ester, Limited, have acquired the business of Messrs. Downes and Co., Nantwich. Downes and Co. were established in the year /865 at Nantwich, and restricted their operations to that town, with the exception of the opening of a sub-branch at the village of Audlem, lying about mid- way between Nantwich and Market Drayton. Although the firm has carried on its business within this limited area, it is very well known among the farmers and others in the district named, and has been well sup- ported by them. The proprietor, Mr. Walter \i,7 Downes, of Stoke Park, Barbridge, Cheshire, and the " Dowery," Nantwich, is now in his eightieth year, and on that account has decided to retire from business. Vie firm's London agents have for many years been the London and West- minster Bank, Limited, London. The Union Bank continues as far as possible the arrangements which existed with the old firm; and Mr. Downes will con- tinue to give the Union Bank's branch manager the benefit of his advice and experience. Thus passes away one of the few left of the old firms of private bankers. The Union Bank was established in 1836, and has its head office in Manchester,_ with branches at Liverpool, Northwich, Middlewich, Winsford, Warrington, Stock- port, Oldham, Blackburn, Burnley, and about seventy other branches in the towns in • the neighbourhood of Manchester and Liverpool. It may be mentioned that the Union Bank some years ago toorover the business of Messrs. J. Sewell and Nephew, Bankers, Norfolk- street, Manchester, and in more recent times has I absorbed the business of Messrs. E. W. Yates and Co., Bankers, Liverpool, and also of the Blackburn Bank, Limited, Blackburn.

A PLEASANT SUNDAY AFTERNOON.

It was the Sabbath, the day of quietude, of rest, of tranquillity : the one day in seven when the worker is free to indulge in that calm intellectual browsing, that easy meander of speculation which is so refresh- ing after one's energies and abilities have been focussed for six days upon the task of earning the daily bread. I had dined well, and seated in an easy chair enjoying the fragrance of a good cigar I was speedily wafted away on the wings of optimism to the groves of A.rcady, the fields of Elysian delight. Through my open lattice was wafted the perfume of , a thousand flowers, to the mellifluent cadence of church bells was added the music of lowing kine and the many subdued notes of bird and insect which blend into a glorious symphony. On the village green the children gambolled like lambs at play, whilst their' elders sat round, the women discussing the subjects nearest their hearts, the men smoking the pipes of peace ; the whole scene was one of calm, of peaceful happiness, one typically English. Alas I was not per- I mitted long to bask in the sunshine of such scenes. I awoke to reality, to Warrington ! From my window I saw a crown of hobbledehoys, potential hooligans, engaged in an exciting game of pitch and toss. The elders stand at their various doors, some in varying , stages of intoxication, smiling approval on the "sport" what. time the youngsters are scaling the "Alps "of' closet roofs or exploring in the dust bins for orange peel and the various tit-bits of garbage. Few of the children have boots, and the clothes of most bespeak an adult origin, and display a degree of decrepitnde difficult to irnagine.The air is languorous ( ? )with the reek of thick twist and foul with blasphemy; children of tender age lisp horrible curses and speak of things which should be to them as a sealed book.

Verily between the ideal and the real there is a yawning chasm, one which it will take much energy, much enthusiasm and much sacrifice to bridge, and the pity of it is that the children, the hope of the future, are degraded, sullied and spoiled, almost from birth. Whilst children are reared in conditions similar to these I have described the future can have no hope. In the grimy courts and the noisome slums of Warrington are to be found hooligans in the making, the physically strong but morally and mentally degener- ate who go to the filling of our gaols and asylums! I am a dreamer I know, but I ask, could not some- thing have been done with that fourpence which has been taken off the rates to save the children? Could , not the money have been used to gradually change their awful environment and give them a chance to become decent citizens? Perhaps Alderman Bennett or some other cheery optimist will answer me. YORKER.

HOW THE POOR LIVE.

The Housing Problem is being contihually brought before the notice of the public, and the following case emphasizes the urgency of co-operative action : At an inquest on the body of a woman named Alice Walker (60), who had lived at a house in Park-lane, Rochdale. The Coroner, referring to the house asked a witness: How much do you give for this dog kennel ?—The Witness : Four shillings a week. Dr. Kerr, who had made a post-mortem examina- tion of the body, said that death was due to alcohol and want of food. The Coroner : Do you think that these places are fit for people to exist in towns like Rochdale? —I do not. The owner of the property was called as a wit- ness. He said he owned fifteen houses in Park-lane, and let them off as furnished rooms. The woman and a man named Doyle paid 4s. a week. The furniture and the bedding belonged to him (the witness.) The Coroner: Do you consider them fit to live in ?—They have been considered fit all along. Have you to have a licence from the Corpora- tion ?—No, they are furnished rooms. The Coroner: 1- wonder, for your own sake, you do not burn these houses down. They are not fit for human beings to live in.—The Corporation ought to buy them. (Laughter.) The Coroner: If the bedding was mino I should be inclined to take it up by the tongs and throw it out (Jurymen: Hear, hear.) The Foreman: How long is it since you were in this house P—l have been inside this week. The Foreman: It is not fit for a dog to live in. The owner was asked whether these rooms were not let as furnished apartments to hood-wink the Corporation, who could not inspect them as they would do common lodging-houses, and he replied that they were suitable for the class of people that inhabited them. The Coroner informed the owner that the jurymen had been in four or five cif these houses and they were disgusted at the shocking state of things revealed. They were all of opinion that the houses ought to be pulled down. The jury returned a verdict that death was due to alcohol and want of food, and added a rider that the Coroner be requested to draw the atten- of the Corporation to the houses and suggesting that they be swept away.

PROFESSIONALISM IN SPORT AND RELIGION

MUNICIPAL TRADING AND THE LOCAL BUDGET.

Many of us were %Tidy glad to hear Alderman Bennett emphasise the gratifying results of municipal trading in Warrington on the occasion of his budget speech: The significance of the figures rather escaped the assembled Councillors but if they will carefully read the statement prepared by the Alderman it will be seen that his criticisms of Lord Avebury and others were timely and fully justified. The noble lord and his friends never seem to tire of disseminating well- known fictions about local debts. They forget how- ever to produce evidence in support of their contention that municipal trading by adding to the debt is responsible for increasing the rates. It has of course precisely the opposite effect. The trading enterprises I meet the debt charges, sinking fund, etc., and pay out surpluses in relief of rates. If the charges made for gas, electricity, markets, or other service, -provide i surpluses then the critics declare the profits to be I artificial. How can this be when municipal charges are always lower than company charges for the same I service ? It is true the municipality has a monopoly in some respects, but there is always an effective public demand for reduction in price of gas, etc., and the public monopoly does not reap huge profits by inflating prices, as would occur if the monopoly were a private one. The monopoly is in the nature of the services, whether owned by the community or by a company. The inhabitants of Warrington are profit makers even when dead, for the local Cemetery has a revenue in excess of expenditure. The traducers of municipal trading, wriggle as they may, cannot explain away the facts compiled so excellently by the Borough Treasurer of Preston. What the public socially need they should socially own and control, and this dictum is proving itself to be a paying basis of municipal finance even in so small a town as War- rington.__ _ _

Long live Ilunicipal enterprise and its prophets, say I.

LAZARUS,

WHY THE L.R.C. HAS FAILED

In my second article under the heading of " Mysterious initials," I gave a few facts concerning the history of the Warrington Labour Representation Committee. It would be seen that up to the time when the Shipwright's executive refused to allow Mr. Wilkie to became the Labour candidate for Warring- ton, the increase in membership had been very satis- factory, and my contention is that had Mr. Wilkie been secured as candidate the membership would have continued to grow and in all probability there would now have been a strong L.R.C. with a representative in Parliament, and also members on the Town Council. The result of having a Parliamentary Labour candi- date has had a remarkable effect in other towns, in building up the local labour rarty, and has been the main factor in keeping the members together. Various objections have been raised against the L.R.C. from time to time by local trades unionists. Strong objec- tion, sometimes very unfair, was made by Liberal trades unionists, against any further. attempt being made to secure a Labour candidate, after failing with Mr. Wilkie. It was argued by the objectors that it would damage Mr. Crosfieldis chances of victory. Why this should concern the members of an L.R.C. I am at a loss to understand, without it is that many of the membe s were Liberals first and trades unionists and Labour men a "long way after." Then objection was made by ' others to the I.L.P. being connected with the Committee. This was because the I.L.P was a Socialist organisation. So strong was the objection that the members of the I.L.P. decided to secede, so that there might be no excuse in that score for trades unionists refusing to to join L.R.C. It was Said that they, the trades unionists, objected to any orgAnisation being affiliated who could put forward candidates for election who I were not trades unionists, and who would have to be supported by all the members. The withdrawal of the I.L.P. however did not prove to be the "turn of the tide "as was expected would be the result. Those of us who have been closely connected with the Labour movement during recent years know that the I.L.P. element has been and is now, the motive power which helps forward Om movement of labour representation. Without the I.L.P. influence, either through the branches or through individual members no L R.C. possesses any vitality worth the name. In my opinion the principal reason for the failure of the L.R.C. in Warrington is the apathy and prejudice of the local trade unionists. This can only be removed by educa- tion on the principles of labour representation. Who is to administer that education if not the 1.L.P.? Can any reader answer the question? I should like to see it answered if there is another method which is a better one. The first lesson is one of political independence, to be followed by the lesson of Socialism as the ideal for all. One must admire the way in which Mr. Buckley, as secretary, has stood by the committee. Ms efforts were certainly deserving of better results.

FROM WARRINGTON TO LONDON.

IThe extraordinary success of municipal trading at Warrrington (says the " Daily New ") furnishes another object lesson for London. The Warrington budget for the coming year shows that the rates are being reduced by 4d. in the pound, owing to the increased profit from town services. The actual amount of relief now received from the municipal undertakings has risen to £16,000 per annum equival- ent to a rate of is. 7d. in the pound. Alderman Bennett, in presenting these cheerful figures to the Towp Council, remarked, amidst cheers, that if War- rington were wound up to-morrow it could pay 265. Bd. in the pound. As time goes on, these results, excellent as they are, must, from the inherent character of the case, be beaten, for Warrington, of course, is not merely making profit on its undertak- ings, but year by year redeeming its capital indebted- ness. Every year the sinking fund reduces the amount of interest to be paid in the succeeding year: So Warrington will pass from strength to strength, while London is unfortunately denied by its own folly the means of acquiring further remunerative undertakings. It has the privilege of bearing all the unremunerative expenditure; but it has votel for handing all the remunerative expenditure over to the companies. It will soon be asked to lease its electric bulk supply to a Trust,to which it will pay toll perhaps for half a century on every tramcar run and every , lamp lit. At the end of that time it will buy out the I scheme at twice its value as it bought out the Water ; Trust only a year or two ago.

writes deprecating professionalism in sport He goes further, however, and regrets that the work- ingmen of Warrington should devote so much of their time and thought to football and so little to religion. "Football," he says, "is now purely professionalism, another and easy way of getting a living without working, and the players play the game because they are paid." A.P.L., I fancy, is one of those individuals who find some pleasure in denouncing professionalism in sport, because football attracts big crowds, whilst the churches remain almost empty. Professionalism in sport, however, ought to be no more denounced than professionalism in religion. We pay men to preach and pray for us, and why should we not pay men to play football for us? If it is possible to get smarter and cleverer men for the ministry by paying them to devote the whole of their services to the profession, why should it not be equally advantageous to pay athletes to give their whole time to sport? Very much of the shouting against this, that, or the other form of amusement comes from sources, I am afraid, not altogether free from selfishness. If we could examine the fly-blown credentials of the grumblers, we should not only find them antiquated, but signed by I COULD ONLY HEAR WHISPERS. carping bigots with hearts no bigger than pea-nuts. If a man is no better for watching of playing repetition of the query by his wife, who, throughout the game he should give it up. WARRINGTON BLUE GOAT SCHOOL.

Presentation to Mr. and Mrs. Rose.

At the meeting of the trustees held on Monday last, an interesting prese itation was made to Mr. and Mrs. Rose, the head master and mistress, in connection with their 25 years of service to the institution. Mr. J. C. Parr was in the chair, and there were also present Canon Willis, Rev. E. C. E. Carleton, Rev. Harry Pigot, .Captain Withington, Messrs. Charles White, James Hepherd, R. C. Parr, George Dakin, R. T. Fairclough, Major Appleton and Colonel Buckton (trustees), Mr. Henry Greenall (law clerk) and Mr. E. J. Hall. Letters expressing regret at their inability to be present were also read from Colonel Blackburne (who was abroad) and Canon..Penrhyn.

Mr. and Mrs. Rose were called into the room, all the trustees standing as they entered when the Chairman said: —" As chairman and senior trustee present here to-day, it is my privilege to convey to you the congratulations of the trustees,with the expression of their satisfaction and approbation of your management of this school and your care of the children during the 25 years you have had it under your charge. At the last meeting when it transpired that a quarter of a century had elapsed since you made your first annual report, it seemed to have occurred to all the trustees present that the event deserved some little recognition, not. in the light of a reward for past services, these we feel sure will reap their reward in the feeling that you have done your duty and earned the esteem and regard of the trustees and the respect and grati- tude-of all connected directly and indirectly with the school, and that you have been instrumental in launch- ing into the world so many of the rising generation equipped with all that good education, good moral training, and•above all good religious. The Examiner, Saturday, April 20, 1907.

MY DEAR RELATIONS,— There is a very old saying that "Spring Showers bring forth May Flowers." This is undeniable, and, as far as I can see, I think that the same joyful season can also be credited with bringing forth a great many new members —and this is a subject very near to my heart. My post this week includes applications from nine would-be members, all of whom are eligi- ble. I only wish this would be the average each week, and in time we should soon become a leading League. As it is, considering the length of our existence we have not done at all badly, as our books show over three thousand members. I shall not be satisfied until we have at least ten thousand, so I hope all you dear children will hurry and bustle about so that my ambition will soon be realised. How are you all getting on with the puzzles? I hope by the time this letter is in print to raceive hundreds of solutions, and, what's more, I believe I shall get them, and I am 'one of those cheerful individuals who live in hope—and trust to luck. Yours till next week, UNCLE NED.

WELCOME TO Edith Wallace. E. Warren. Doris Exley. Joseph Harper. Florence Eileen Hays. D'Arcy Betts. Ethel Collett. Fred Joy. Toni Edmund Jessop.

think the matter over. Gerty T.—Thanks for letter received. I hope to answer it fully in my next.

DR. AGAR BEEr'S VISIT.

Lecture on " Nature and God."

The Rev. Dr. Agar Beet is one of the best known theologians of the day and his visit to Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church on Sunday and Monday aroused con- siderable interest. The rev, gentleman formerly worked in the Warrington circuit and has many friends here who were pleased to take the opportunity of hear- ing him. There were large congregations on Sunday and Dr. Beet's eloquent and instructive discourses were listened to with deep attention. The choir rendered special music, Mr. T. W. Pearce being at the organ. At the morning service Miss Hipkiss sang "Come unto me" very effectively. In the evening the choir gave a creditable interpretation of CTiounod's anthem Send out Thy light "and Miss Pitt feelingly sang " 0 rest in the Lord."

"NATURE AND GOD." DR. BEET'S INTERESTING LECTURE.

On Monday evening there was a largii congregation .to hear a lecture by Dr. Beet on "Nature and God." Mr. James Dodds was announced to preside, but he was unable to attend as he was away from home recruiting his health. The Rev. Sidney Pitt, who took the chair, said all the members of that church had 'a heartfelt wish that Mr. Dodds' health which was so valuable to them and to so many might be completely restored, and that quickly. Tn the course of a letter Mr. Dodds said "I think it wIl that in this passing hour of spiritual unrest a tried, trusted, competent theological leader like Dr. Beet,so ripe in scholarship, so clear in thought, so transparently fearless and honest, should come down to Warrington occasionally and in our presencerifeel about the bases and foundation of our faith and still more firmly stablish our hearth and minds in the great Christian verities." Mr. Dodds had for- warded a substantial contribution to the collection. The Chairman went on :to speak of the value at this time of having amongst them a teacher so clear and reliable as Dr. Beet. It was a great pleasure to see so many youngf people present, and he hoped they would all possess themselves of Dr. Beet's recent book "The Compendium of Theology." They would then see how very far their faith was from resting on insecure founda- tions, how little it was that wan taken for granted, and how step by step the way was made on which a reason- able faith might rest. Dr. BEET said his topic that night was two great words—" Nature" and God," and he purposed to try to go down to the broad ultimate foundations not so much of Christianity .but of all .religions and to go hack to something older than Christ or even than Moses or the earliest part of the Old Testament, to something ..vider-spreading than Christianity.He wanted to try to fiud certain solid foundations on which all religious people of every religion might set their feet and stand firmly. They would look first at religion without Ohrist and without the Bible. When he said

that he meant a religion available for many who lived before Christ was born and who never saw the Bible or beard the name of Moses. They had no bigger words in human language than the words " Nature " and God." Nature included everything they could see and touch and feel by direct perception. The name of God described One whom they could neither see, nor touch, One great Invisible who surrounded and embraced everything. He thought they would find written on the face of Nature the name of God. When they stood before the God of Nature they would find themselves in a great dillicultl and dilemma, but in that difficulty the name of Jesus Christ and His personal reality would come to them and do for them what Nature itself could not do. The great and wonderful picture of the world was the first thing they saw on looking around them, and they noticed how many of the objects In nature were of great beauty, whilst others showed a marvellous adaptation of means to useful ends. Looking at nature more discriminatingly,they saw that everything divided itself into three categories which be could bring before them in very small compass in the form of the rock, the moss that grew upon the rock, and the butterfly. Those three represented the categories of the lifeless, the living and the rational. Another fact which Impressed them was that changes were always going on, and some of them, such as day and night, the seasons, youth, old age, and death were rhythmic changes. They wanted to find the ulti- mate source of nature. The ocean, the stars, and the rooks—where did they come from? Fortunately to some exteut they could answer that question and here natural science came to their help. He was not going to contradict natural science—he was not fit to do so— and he would not accept anything from natural science except those points in which all men of science were agreed. One of those points of agreement was that there were the three divisions of the lifeless,the living, and the rational. Those divisions marked off three great epochs in the world's history. Every man of science would tell them that `there wfis a time when nothing lived on earth, when this planet was lifeless. Then again there was a time when there were living things on earth, but no man or woman. Another result of science which they owed to the last century was the knowledge of the fact that so far as science had observed, out of the living the lifeless never came. But it had come, because this was once a lifeless world and to-day it teemed with life. Within the limits of these three epochs, men of science could do a great deal to tell them how things came to be. They told them that the lower animals existed first and by means of diagrams they showed plainly bow the successively higher forms came into being. Purther than that ,men of science had tried to explain how our planet came into existence. He (the lecturer)thought they had shown suecessfully that there were certain natural forces at work and that those

forces made the big ball of a planet on which we lived. Darwin and others had tried to show that in a somewhat similar way by the operation of natural forces the successive forms of animals' one higher than the other, had come into being. But they had utterly failed to show how life came to be. He thought they had given that up. No natural force known to us could create life. No one could explain the functions of life, the mysterious faculty with which a human being took food into himself and in the wonderful laboratory of ' his own body turned it into bone, flesh and blood. There must have been at work, in a time *(roue by a force altogether higher than all the forces that man had observed in nature. They found in nature the footprint of a greater power. In all ages,continued the lecturer, men, had seen that this universe must spring from intelli- gence. Science had been a wonderful handmaid to theology and they must wish well to their men of science in prosecuting their studies. All_natural _ _

science told them that the universe was one. Whatever was known stood related to whatever else was known. The lecturer then referred to conscience, showing how

men pronounced judgment as to what was right and wrong and even condemned their own actions. While pronouncing that judgment they knew that not only was it just but that it was the voice of someone higher than themselves. These judgments were practically the same in all extreme cases, in all ages and in all nations. In the literature of the ancient world, written before Christ by people who had never . . .

heard-the name of Moses, they iouild the same persons were honoured and the same sort were dishonoured.

There was, continued the lecturer, a certain grim satis- faction when a bad man got hi u deserts, even though it was at the hands of the assassin. We did not excuse the assassin but we felt that the fact that bad men killed one another made them the executioners of God to some extent. On the whole sin brought sorrow and icell-doing brought happiness. There were always exceptions which had puzzled the wisest of mankind in all ages. The good man who did right and lost his life

had fast all unless there was some-thing beyond the grave. In all ages of ,the world the same explanation had been given—that the present life was not the only life, that death was not the end of all, that beyond the grdve the martyr still lived, receiving the due reward for the righteous act which brought him to a premature grave. They found permeating all the literature of the ancient world a _ _

belief in an ultimate retribution for good and evil. This .was the Universal Theology—an intelligent Creator,

a righteous Ruler, a retributiOi; beyond the-grave. That was the solid foundation. The Moslems believed all that. Natural Theology, however, could not save; they ne3ded more than that, because they had sinned and when be thought of judgment he was not prepared to stand and be judged, for he had sinned. Natural theology had no word of pardon and therefore it could not save. Unless they could teach children something more than to pay their debts, they would not save them. They needed the word of pardon and the power to do right, and that was where Jesus Christ came in. All these things were preparing the way for Mtn by creat- ing in man the sense of guilt and helplessness, and then Jesus came and said Come unto me all ye that have erred and are weary, and I will give you rest." That was why they sent missionaries to the heathen. While they taught the happier doctrines of the Gospel they must firmly teach the plain fundamentals which had been their matter of study that evening. On the mption of the Rev. SIDNEY PITT, a cordial vote of thanks was passed to Dr. Beet for his lecture and to Mr. Dodds for his help in arranging the visit.

BATTLESHIP COALING RECORD.

H.M.S. Implacable broke the world's record at Malta on Thursday, taking on board 1,050 tons or coal in two hours and forty minutes, being an average of 394 tons per hour. No shore labour was engaged, and only baskets were used. The record was previously held by H.M. cruiser Black Prince, with 316 tons per hour.

"TOO LAZY TO WALK."

Said to be too lazy to walk, Arthur Chilcot, a drowsy- looking individual, also proved himself too lazy to talk at the South-Western Police'Court on Thursday, when he was sentenced to threh months' hard labour as a refractory casual, with the expression of a magisterial hope that he would be made to work in prison.

42 YEARS AS BANDMASTER. I A concert was held in the Newton Town Hall on Monday night to commemorate the 42 years' service of Bandmaster T. Batley. The selections by the band were well received, and the programma was also sus- tained by Miss Tissie Eden, Mr. J. Mchoßs, Mr. W. E. Forshaw, the Leo Male Voice Choir and Mr. John Molyneux.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l should very much like to become one of your Nieces. I am sending you a story, .entitled, "The First Echo." THE FIRST ECHO.

Long, long ago the Goddess Juno became very angry with Echo, one of her maidens. Echo was a lively girl, always bright and cheerful, and very fond of talking. In order to punish her, Juno took. from the girl the power of speech, allowing her only to repeat the last word spoken by others. At the same time there lived a very beautiful, but very vain and selfish, boy, named Narcissus. One day he lost him- self in the woods, and, hoping someone was near, he cried out, "Is anyone here?" Echo, who was resting under a tree, wished to speak to this handsome youth, but could say only, -Here." Narcissus looked around, but seeing no one, passed on to a clear, cool pond. Being thirsty, ho stooped to drink, when he saw a picture of his own face in the water. "Who are you?" he asked in surprise. Echo, hidden among the trees, answered, "You."

The vain Narcissus smiled, and the face in the water smiled back at him. It was such a beautiful face that he could not tear himself from the spot. . .

Day after day he knelt beside the water look- ing at himself. "Speak to me," he cried again and again, and every time poor Echo replied, "To me." He became thin and weak, and at his great grief killed him. The body of the youth became changed into a 'beautiful flower—the lovely narcissus which to this day you will find growing by the sides of lakes of the woods. As for poor Echo, when the found that Narcissus did not speak again, she grew troubled. Little by little her body faded away, until at last nothing was left of the once lovely girl but her voice. E. WARREN.

TOLL PRY.

ein going to tell you a sad story about a Ale girl whose nick-name was "Poll Pry." the was, on the whole, a good girl; but she Lad one great fault. Her mother was a dress- maker, and, when alady came to try on a dress, she was always sent out of the room(' This the little girl did not like; and she always stood just outside, and peeped through. the keyhole. One day her father came in at the back-door, and saw his little daughter with her eye at the keyhole of the parlour door. He told her how wrong it was for anyone to do things on the sly. But the next time she had the chance she did the same thing again. One day a lady came, and wished to see her mother quite alone. So she was sent out of the room, and told to shut the door after her. But she took care not to Latch, the door, as she wanted to hear what was said. When the lady saw the door was not quite closed, she walked across the room and shut it. At that moment, a dread- ful scream was heard on the other side of the door; and, on opening it, there was the poor girl with her finger all black and bloody. She I was holding the door ajar when the lady came and shut it, and so her finger was jammed. It was a long time before she lost the name of "Poll Pry," but from that day she was cured of her fault. Whenever she was tempted to look or listen on the sly, she thought of her jammed finger. Hoping you will like my story,

MARGARET LILIAN PAYNE,

M. WADSWORTH. When is an apple like a fish? When is a man like a tea-kettle just on the boil? Why is two thirteens like half a crown?

CLEVER FRED. Fred came home from school the first half-year, As learned as learned could be: And wished to show to all around

How sharp a boy was he. So when at dinner he began: "Papa, you think you see Two roasted chickens on that dish; Now I can prove them three. "First this is one and that is one,

As plain as plain can be. I add the one unto the two And two and one make tree." "If so, then," answered his Papa, "If what you say is true, I'll take one, mamma take one; The third we'll leave for you!" ETHEL COLLETT.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l wish to become a member of your Children's Corner, so I enclose a fctory, called,

NOT FINGERS .ENOUGH

A little boy was once sent to school, when he was only four years old. He was very good, and tried to learn all he could; but, of course, at first he was not able to do much, and teacher only gave him easy Lessons. Among other things, she taught him how to do addition sums, and told him to use his fingers in count- ing. This he did for some time, until he got quite quick at adding numbers up to ten. But one day he was set a sum that came to more than ten. He could not make it out, and began tl try. "What is the matter, Bobby?" asked his teacher. "Please," Bobby replied, "I have not fingers enough to do this sum 1"

FLORENCE EILEEN HAYS.

===== 0002601_19070420_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
VISITORS

When little boys come in to lunch, And stay till after tea, My mother says, "How kind they are To come and play with me." They take my marbles, break my toys; And, when I want to fight, My mother says, "They're visitors, And you must be polite." "Give George the whip," she says, "And you can be the horse," And "He must have the biggest pear," And "be the King, of course." When we are hunting, he's the " cat," I'm just the frightened mouse— I'd rather be the visitor , At someone else's house.

MAY BRIGGS.

MAKING CAKES. I really love to be about On Mother's baking day, And coax for just a piece of dough, At making cakes to play. Some currants and some candied peel, To make my cakes so nice; And, just to see that they afre right,

I taste them once or twits. I cut the peel in tiny bits, I stick the currants in; And then I bang the mixture hard With Mother's rolling-pin. But somehow it surprises me,

When both the cakes are thine— My Mother's is as light as air, And mine a, heavy one. DORIS EXLEY.

THE NORTHERN UNION CUP. Warrington Finalists Again

SWINTON OUTCLASSED AT WIGAN.

Warrington made no mistake on Saturday, about securing tile right of contesting the final tie for the Cup when they easily vanquished Swinton in the semi-final at Wigan. A keen contest was expected but Warrington ran the Lions off their feet and won easily by 21 points to nil. Notwithstanding the wet morning there was a gate of 12,000 people, a quarter of whom came from Warrington, and the receipts were £328. Warrington played as selected, but Swinton had to substitute Oldfield for Fielding at full-back. The ground was in fair condition, though soft. Teams and officials : Warrington—Tilley, back; Jordan, Taylor, Isherwood, and Fish, three-quarter backs: Lees and Hockenhall, half backs ; Heath, Boardman, Thomas, Shugars, Belton, and Harmer, forwards. Swinton—Oldfield, back; Griffin, Valentine, Wall- work, and Jones, three-quarter backs; Booth and Cowell, half backs; Simister, Barnett, Davies, Hobson, Pomfret and Lloyd, forwards. Referee, Mr. W. Robinson, Manningham. • Warrington were fortunate in winning the toss: for it gave them the advantage of a strong breeze during the first half, but it was their opponents who made all the running in the initial sages. Swinton's forwards led off with a tremendous rush that gave an earnest of the policy they intended to pursue. It was their purpose to keep the ball close, and they succeeded admirably for a time. Tilley was hard pressed and Fish came to the relief of his side at a critical moment. A tre- mendous punt by him found touch over three parts of the field distipt and some vigorous play was witnessed IT the Swinton quarter. Lees, Hockenhall, Taylor and Jordan exchanged smartly, and a try seemed certain when the whistle went for a forward transfer. Still, Warrington were getting the upper band, and a premature attempt at clearance by the Swinton forwards resulted in a penalty from which Fish opened the score with a nice goal. Cowell was next forced to fall on the ball near his own line from , the scrummage. Lees and Isherwood handled smartly, ' Fish taking the final transfer, and running past Jones with ease. No goal resulted, but for the next tea minutes there was a perfect fusilade of penalty kicks , against the rash Swintonians, and Fish hit either the ' costs or the cross-bar four times without getting the all over the bar. Harmer and Heath dribbled over the line, only to be called back for an informolity, and Fish on another occasion cross-kicked to Jordan, who was only held a yard short of the line. But the " Wire- pullers " were not to be denied, and just before the interval Fish, after taking a pass from Isherwood doubled in, and caused a scrummage to be formed near the line, from which Lees broke away, and scored another unconverted try. Thus at half-time the score stood :—Warrington 1 goal 2 tries (8 points), Swinton nil. The second half went entirely in favour of Warring- ton who scored within two minutes of the re-start. The score was practically given away by Oldfield, who failed completely to field a bounding ball, and let in Thomas, who had nothing to do but pick up the ball and register a try, which Fish failed to convert. The " Lions " yielded absolutely after this, and very feeble, indeed, were the efforts made by their backs to defend their line. Warrington made all the play, and that they did not score more than they actually did was probably due tn the fact they that could see they bad their opponents at their mercy. After a lot of by-play, during which the ball was tossed about more or less indiscriminately, Hockenhall cleverly dropped a goal, and this effort he followed up with a smartly obtained try. The try wa in a measure due to Fish who had with fine judg- ment, kicked across the field, and the latter on this occasion added the goal points. The last score was a try credited to Fish, who romped over at the corner after the ball had passed through the hands of Thomas and Lees, and so the same had the result :—Warrington 3 goals,s tries (21 points), Swinton nil.

===== 0002601_19070420_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
SALFORD FAIL AGAIN.

The weather was worse at Rochdale, but nearly 16,000 onlookers paid to see the semi-final tie of the Northern Union Cup between Oldham and Salford, on the ground of the Rochdale Club. The receipts were £450.

Lomas won the toss, and, with a great wind blowing straight down the ground,naturally made Ferguson kick off against it. Oldham, however, soon showed they knew how to adapt themselves to the conditions, and, ably led by A. Lees, they neutralised the Salford men's advantage as much as it could possibly be done. The backs rarely kicked; if they could not make ground by low passing they preferred to be tackled, whilst the forwards dribbled most effectively, and were very difficult to stop. Ten minutes from the start Lewis,one of Salford forwards,was ordered off the field for striking an opponent,Mr.Clegg having only a few minutes previously warned him. After this Salford repeatedly got to the Oldham line by wind-aided kicks, but they could not keep their position. Preston and John were greatly at fault with wild passing,and when the three-quarters did receive the ball they generally dropped it, the only ex- ception to this rule being W. Thomas. Most of the good football came from the Oldharn men, and Mason was only just able to stop one capital round by Lees, the two Dixons, and Tyson from yielding a try. When the teams crossed over at half-time nothing had been scored, but Oldham went right away from the restart, and scored a splendid try. White was the trygetter, but the ball had previously been through the hands of Yewlett, Tooham, Lees, and W. Dixon. Cochrane made a bie attempt to stop White, but did not quite succeed. No goal was kicked, and Ferguson twice failed with penalty kicks, as Lomas had done for Salford in the opening half. Oldham continued to have all the best of the game. Salford were very hard pressed, but defended stoutly, and kept their opponents from scoring. Ultimately, however, Brown foolishly handled a long punt instead of allowing the ball to go dead, and from a subsequent scrummage on the Salford line Avery scored as a result of a scramble. Ferguson failed at goal, and thereafter Oldham had all the game, but they could not increase their lead, though the result was never in doubt. Result :—Oldham 2 tries (6 points), Salford nil.

RUGBY UNION.

STOCKTON HEATH v. ROCHDALE.

At Stockton Heath on Saturday. Rochdale started, a good return being made by Steele. Stockton Heath indulged in some smart passing, and Warren scored a try, the goal kick failing. Shortly afterwards Warren again got over,and this time Steele failed with the kick. Interval score: Stockton Heath, six points : Rochdale, nothing. In the secisnd half Stockton Heath immedi- ately pressed, but Rochdale relieved and took play to the other end, where Ray scored a try, the goal kick failing. Roe next got over, Tweedy placing a goal. In the last few minutes Barker added another for Rochdale. Final: Rochdale, eleven points; Stockton Heath, six points.

The Examiner, Saturday, April 20, 1907.

SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

SOLIDIFIED TAR.

A process has been introduced for solidify- ing gas-tar, so that the material can be made into blocks, which are much hattdier for transportation than the liquid material, in ,casks. The tar is subjected to a process of distillation, whereby about 4 per cent. of the total weight of the constituents passes off. This 4 per cent. is composed of light oils and crude naphtha, which. are Used for gas en- richment. It is claimed that the solidified material will be very useful for road-making.

===== 0002601_19070420_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WORLD'S BED ROCK

Granite is the bed rock of the world. It is the lowest rock in the earth's crust, and shews no sign of animal life. It is from two to ten times as thick as all the other layers of rocks combined. No evidences of life of either animal or vegetable are apparent in granite. * The presence of lime is due to animal life. Some experts assert that all the lime in the world has at some time been a part of living bodies, including human beings.

===== 0002601_19070420_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
A TRANSPARENT MIRROR

mirror which will give an image in the ordinary way, but will be transparent then observed by transmitted light, can be made of platinised glass, i.e., the glass is metalised with platinum. Such a mirror may be set in a frame with a panel behind it with an aper- ture closed by a shutter. If the shutter is closed one may look at oneself in the ordi- nary way; if the shutter is. open, anything placed there—a portrait or 'an image—will appear instead of one's own presentment.

===== 0002601_19070420_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SURVIVAL OF. THE UNFIT.

Whilst there is a certainty of man's power to remove all disease from his life, a diffi- culty which he has, already created for him- self will be thereby increased. That difficulty, writes Professor E. Ray Lankester in "The Kingdom of Man." is the increase of human populati2n beyond the capacity of the earth's surface lo provide food and the other necessi- ties of life. By rebelling against Nature's method, man has made himself the only ani- mal which constantly increases in, numbers. Whenever disease is controlled his increase will be still more rapid than at present. At the same time no attempt has yet been made by the more advanced Ammunities of civilised men to prevent the multiplication of the weakly or of those liable to congenital disease.

===== 0002601_19070420_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
I SCIENTIFIC SECRET.

An interesting paper was read at a meet- ing of the French Academy of Medicine as to a curious property discovered as latent in the amalgam of platinum. It is known that mer- cury in emulsion with water separates itself from the latter as soon as the tube in which they are contained ceases to be agitated. It is not thus with the amalgam of platinum. The mercury holding platinum in solution is r.ttracted when acted upon some seconds with water. It forms a metallic mass which re- sists the action of heat or void, betraying no change under 100 deg. of heat, and capable of being cut into slices for microscopic examination at 80deg. below zero. M. Mois- san discovered this property, but died before he could determine its explanation, which is still under question.

===== 0002601_19070420_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
THE AGE OF ANIMALS.

Some of the compound earth worms that have been produced at the Marburg Zoologi- cal Institute by grafting together parts of different worms have lived from eight to ten years after the operation. It is fair, says Pro- fessor Korscholt, to infer that uncombined earth worms ,may reach the same age, a fact that was unsuspected and would have been deemed improbable. This discovery suggests a comparison with the ages of other inverte- brates for which, however, few reliable data are available. Among the generally short- lived articulate animals lobsters and fresh- water crabs are said to live twenty years, queen bees five -Years, and queen ants from ten to fifteen years. Most molluscs, too, appear to be short-lived, but the fresh-water mussel is said to live twelve or fourteen years (the pearl-bearing species more than fifty years, according to some writers), this sea snail, Natica. thirty years, and the Tridacna, or "yellow ,clam," 100 years. But the life of most invertebrates of all classes appears to be limited to one or a very few years. Hence it is the more surprising to find several well- authenticated

EXAMPLES OF LONGEVITY .;mong the lowly, plantlike sea anemones, of. which on species has been known to live in, aquariums sixty-seven years and others from ten to fifty years. It is impossible, in the • pre- sent state of science, to explain these remark- able exceptions to the law which governs nearly-related animals living under similar condition's. The sany_,, difficulty confronts us in the vertebrates. The great age of ele- phants (150 to 200 years) finds a plausible explanation in the.ir great size and the un- favourable conditions for Procreation, but there is no known explanation for the ages attained by some birds (the raven 100, the eagle 104, the vulture 118, the falcon 164, and the parrot a far greater number of years). The longevity of the giant tortoises, which are said to live 300 year 3- may. also be ex- plained by, their bulk and their small expendi- ture of energy', but it is difficult to see why such small vertebrates as pike and carp should live as long as elephants, or toads as long as horses (forty years).

===== 0002601_19070420_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
EXAMINATION OF THE BLOOD.

The microscopical examination of the .blood has become in recent - years a, very common procedure in many cases •of disease. By it the physician. or surge & often obtains valu- able assistance in arriving at a knowledge of the nature of the _disease 'which he is called upon to treat.

rre wood is examined with a view chiefly to ascertain the number, absolute and rela- tive, of the red and white corpuscles, and the percentage of hemoglobin, or colouring-mat- ter, ,of the blood. The red blood-corpuscles are, vastly more numerous than the white, about 5,000,000, speaking generally, being found in one cubic millimetre of blood. Of course one could not count so many, but the approximate result is obtained by counting the number in a determined fraction of a cubic millimetre. • The same method is fol- lowed in counting the white corpuscles, of which there lire -from 500 to 10,000 in a cubic millimetre of blood. In aummia the number of

===== 0002601_19070420_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
RED CORPU3CLES

is markedly decreased, and they are paler in Colour, sliewing a diminution in the -amount Of hemoglobin. In the disease known as per- nicious an:cmia, the average size of the red corpuscles is greatly increased, and there is also a curious irregularity in size, some being twice the normal size while others are found not more than half as large as they should be in health. .

It is in the matter of the actual and propor- tionate number of , the white corpuscles that the most valuable information is furnished by blood examinations. It has been found that the white . cells are not all of the same kind. Six or sevea varieties are found in the blood in health and several others in certain diseases. These various kinds are distin- guished Iv their size,, by their peculiarities of coloration when treated with varioy dyes, n,nd by differences in their granular contents. 'When the white corpuscles are increased in number the condition is called

===== 0002601_19070420_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
LEUCOCYTOSIS,

leucocyte being the .name of the white blood- corpuscle. Leucocytosis occurs in health dur- ing digestion, after exercise or bathing. It is fo,.ind in inflammatory conditions, in certain intectious diseases, in cases of poisoning, in cancer, and in some forms of anremia. The relative proportion of • the different sorts of leucocytes also varies in these affec- tions, and it is by a study of these that the physician is often . enabled to . decide as to which of two or three possible diseases he has to do with in the case before him. . This science of blood examination promises to be of great value to the physician as ex- perience and knowledge increase.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication must in al 1 cases send their names and addresses to the Editor aa a guarantee of good faith.

===== 0002601_19070420_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
" Neutral in all Matters, Political and Religious."

Sir,—The above title has been the proud boast of your contemporary for a generation or two. To many thinking men it has seemed to/ have been an impossible position for anyone to have taken up, especially in the role of a newspaper—but still one must accept the state- ment—and let me say it, to the credit of your con- temporary, that at times it played this difficult role pretty well—though some of us did think occasionally it was very watery and tinged with the hue of an old fashioned Conservatism. But alas ! can we expect,Mr. Editor, your proud contemporary any longer to be "neutral in all matters,political and religious ?" Is it not putting too much of a strain on human nature, to ask us to believe that the role can be played any longer? We notice, with regret some of us, the change in the names of the directors,and especially when we notice the part these gentlemen play in the political and religious circles of the town. As you cogently pointed out in your lively and satirical article the other day on this subject, there is no disguise on the part of these gentlemen as to their Opinions on the political and religiousquestions of tho day. Nay more—they are the leaders, the officials and the directors of the policy of the Conservative Associa- tion of the town and therefore, how in the name of commonsense can they expect the public of Warrington to believe that they have bought up the " Guardian " to run it, so as to be "Neutral in all matters political and religious." If so, they are asking the public of Warrington to swallow that which they are unable to digest. Pardon me, if I should smile at this stage of the proceedings—a great smile.—Yours in sorrow add grief,

"OBSERVER."

===== 0002601_19070420_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
WINWICK FREE CHURCH. Anniversary Celebrations. WHAT CONSTITUTES A CHURCH ?

The Winwick Free Church, opened at the instance of 1 the Warrington Free Church Council, has completed the second year of its existence and anniversary services were held on Sunday. There were good congregations and the discourses of the Rev. J. F. Matthews, who occupied the pulpit morning and evening, were listened to with considerable interest. The evening service was made unusually attractive by the rendering of a solo "A dream of Paradise." by Miss Boardman • Mr. Jones also contri- buted the solo, "Lead kindly light " accompanied by Mr. Morris, on the organ. The choir sang the anthem, "Jerusalem my Glorious Home." At the close of the evening service, Mr. Matthews administered the Sacrament of the Lord's Supper to a large number of communicants, On Thursday evening the annual meeting was held, Mr. C. E. Parker presiding over a small attendance. Prior to the meeting a public tea was held. The Chairman said that perhaps they at Winwick were apt to think they were somewhat of a feeble folk but he wanted them to recognize that they were a section of a great Christian Church that Christ came' to found on this earth. The fact that Christ was the founder gave the Church its abiding character. They thought of many movements in history which had occupied a large part in the thought and life of the nation—such as the different revolutions, the Wars of the Roses, the 'Corn Law and Chartist agitations—and which, having served their purpose had passed away. But the Church of Christ was founded upon His finished work and would abide for ever, until He presented His Church, His Bride, to God the Father. If they studied history they would recognize the fact that only when the , Church Was active and pure was the life of the nation progressive and active. At the Reformation in the sixteenth century England shook off the galling gower of Roman Catilolicism and men recognized the freedom of their souls and the right that every man, without the intervention of a priest, should be able to approach the Father in Heaven. With that Reformation, not only did there come the invigorating power to the Church but it reflected itself in the life of the nation, and so they had after the Reformation a marvellous advance in all that made for the greatness of the country. England progressed in art, science, philosophy and literature, and always after a revival of the Church they saw national progress. When they passed on to the birth of the Puritan movement they reached the period which was the birthplace of the Free Churches. It was at that time that the Baptists and Independents sprang into being and the great thing that characterised the movement was that everyone should be at liberty to worship God as his conscience dictated, without any authority from the State or the King. Although the Puritan movement did very much to liberate conscience it did not altogether win the country to their idea of thinking and they found during the 17th century that the nation slipped back again and the power of the pulpit became very feeble. The upper classes scoffed I at religion and the lower classes wallowed in sin. I But the time came when there was another revival and men like John and Charles! Wesley, George Whitfield, and Fletcher took part in the great Methodist movement. When the Church of Christ was exerting its rightful influence on the life of the nation, then it was that the nation advanced. They of the Free Churches of to-day claimed to be the inheritors of the privileges, the liberties and the free- dom for which the Free Churchmen of old fought. Let them be sure that they maintained that liberty of con- , science, those rights, and freedom which their fathers won. He trusted so far as the Winwick Free Church was concerned, the spirit of Christ might permeate , every member—the spirit of love, truth, faith and hope ; —and if those things abounded, they would be able to form a strong Church that would be a centre from which would radiate all good influences to the village. Mr. Parker proceeded to refer to the Congo atrocities and concluded by ; moving the following ' resolutions :—" That this meeting of Winwick Free Churchmen greatly deplores the horrible atrocities perpetrated on the Congo under the Sovereign rule of Leopold 11., King of the Belgians. It implores his Majesty's Government to take such measures as shall (I) remove the control of that region from the single rule of King Leopold; (2) secure the observance of the spirit of the Berlin Convention; (3) obtain for the native races the right to live and work freely." Mr. Clay seconded, and the resolution was carried unanimously. Mr. E. J. Guest said that hall was a monument to al movement which had been in existence for some years, I which had done a great amount of good, and which I was destined to do still more in the future. He noticed I their motto was "All one in Christ." They trusted the time was coming when they would all be one in Christ. A simple definition of a church was: "Where two or three are gathered together in My name, there am I iiil the midst of them." They believed that where there I were a number of believers together, sincere true believers in the Lord Jesus Christ, they might realise i that He, the Head of the Church, was with them and that they constituted a Church. Theyhoped some time that there would be a broader unity than there was at present, The Free Churches were drawing nearer and ' nearer, but they were sorry that there was a barrier between the Free Churches and the Established Church. They wished that barrier was broken down and they were Berry that Anglicans could not at present realize that Nonconformists were a Church. Anglicans believed that the very essence of the Church was shat there should be Bishops, priests and deacons. He thought that had never been proved to be an essential of the Church. Christ never formulated a Church on those lines, indeed he (the speaker) did not think their Saviour even formulated a visible Church here upon earth. The glory of the old dispensation, whioh was an external glory, had passed away, and the glory of the new dis- pensation was the glory of character; the law written upon the heart, a union and fellowship with God, a coming into the presence of the Father through Christ. The mode in which they worshipped, the ritual and ceremony, were matters of detail. If they worshipped sincerely and in truth it mattered not what their ministers were designated or what the style of their Church might be—it might be Gothic or barn-like, a Cathedral or the plainest place on earth—but if the Spirit of the Lord was there they had liberty to worship the Father in spirit and in truth. They had no antag- onism to the Anglican or Roman Catholic Churches. They believed they were doing a great work. But they wished they would realize that Free Churchmen had a share in that great work, that they were workers together with them, and co-workers with God in the evangelisation of the world. (Applause). Mr. R. Ormiston presented a report on the year's work which, he said, had been very successful. On the whole they had had splendid preachers. At the last anniversary they raised ZIO, and in addition they had received during the year £24 15s. &I. from collections and subscriptions. The expenditure totalled £l2 19s 6d. They trusted the present membership. would be the nucleus of a strong Church. They had not the slightest grudge against the Anglican Church in the village, and he did not think the Anglican Church had much grudge against them. He hoped they would rally round the members in a special effort to reduce the debt on the building of £lOO. The Chairman pointed out that the Free Church Council seriously recognized its respon.sibility with ' regard to the finances of the Church. The Rev. J. F. Matthews said he hoped the members of the church would look upon Mr. Parker, their new I president, as their honorary pastor. They could not have a better president for he was always willing to do his utmost to further the work of the Christian Church. During the past two years he (Mr. Matthews) had ; supplied 117 preachers to the pulpit and now that the ' people of Winwick had to undertake that duty them- selves he trusted they would have the help of some of the best preachers that the Free Churches of Warrin - I ton could supply. He hoped they Would soon seriousfr I consider the advisability of forming a Sunday Sono° I (Applause.)

TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCIL.

MR. A H. CROSFIELD, M.P., AND THE BAKERS' EIGHT HOURS BILL.

"Empire Day" and Jingoism.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council was held in Bold-street Wesleyan school on Tuesday evening, under the presidency of Mr. T. Plumb. There was a fair attendance. THE LATE EX-COUNCILLOR WRIGHT.

The CFIAIRMAN made a sympathetic allusion to the late ex-Councillor Wright who, he said, had served Labour and the town exceedingly well. He was straightforward and honest and all deeply regretted his death at such an early age. The chairman concluded by moving a vote of condolence with the widow and family. Mr. RANDLES seconded and referred to the excellent reports presented by Mr. Wright on the work of the Town Council.

Mr. GREALEY and Councillor STOTT supported the motion which was passed in silence. WITHDRAWAL OF A SOCIETY.

A letter was read from the Secretary of the Warring- ton Branch of the Friendly society of Ironfounders of England, Ireland and Wales, intimating that the mem- bers had decided to withdraw from the Council. As there was no rule requiring notice from societies with- drawing he took it that their membership ceased immediately. Councillor STOTT thought they ought not to let the ironfounders leave • the Council without making an effort to retain them. _

It was decided to ask the branch to receive a deputa- tion consisting of the chairman, the secretary (Mr. Plinston) and Councillor Stott to induce them to reconsider their decision. SCHOLARSHIPS FOR THE WORKING CLASSES. A circular was received drawing attention to the cobden Club's proposal to interest the industrial classes in economic questions. The Club offer to members of trade unions, co-operative societies and similar industrial organizations in Manchester and district who belonged to the wage-earning class, a scholarship and four prizes to be awarded to the candi- dates who shall acquit themselves best in a written examination, one paper being on general economics and the other on Free Trade.

Councillor STOTT expressed the hope that young men connected with trade unions would take up the scheme as it would be to their own advantage and to the benefit of others to whom they could impart imformation.

===== 0002601_19070420_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
ELECTION PROSPECTS.

Mr. J. Rctmsay Macdonald wrote on behalf of the Labour Party urging the importance of registration work being speedily undertaken by labour organizations. It was, he said, evident that if the House of Lords was to take up a strong attitude of antagonism to the Government this year, an appeal to the country in 1908 would be a reasonable possibility. Greater efforts should be made to retreive the position of Labour on local bodkes. Mr. RA&DLES said the Conservatives and Liberals were doing their utmost to organize their forces against Labour and it behoved the Labour party to take the field at once. He believed there were even delegates to that Council who needed educating politically, as they would probably vote against Labour and in favour of Liberals and Con- servatives.

===== 0002601_19070420_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. CROSFIELD M.P., AND BAKERS' HOURS. The SECRETARY read the following letter, dated from Hotel Belle Vue, Cannes, April 6th :—Mr. A. H. Crosfield is obliged to you for your favour of the 2nd, bringing before his notice the draft Bill to restrict the hours of labour in bakehouses. While in sympathy with the principle of the Bill as drafted, Mr. Crosfleld does not at this stage feel himself justified iu giving a definite promise of support before hearing the argu- ments which may be advanced for or against the measure when it is brought up for discussion in the House. The resolution included in your letter is duly noted by Mr. Crosfleld. You will doubtless have seen in the papers that Mr. Burns is dealing energetically with this question. Mr. GREALEY said the Bill would come before the House this week. Mr. Crosfield's reply was not very definite.

The CA.AIRMAN : It is not definite that he will support it, but it seems to me to be definite the other way—that he won't. Councillor STOTT said that as to the reference to the unemployed question contained in the latter part of Mr. Crosfleld's letter, be thought Mr. Burns had been dealing with some Labour men more energetically than he bad been dealing with the unemployed question. It was the Labour party who had been dealing with the matter, and he thought it would not have been men- tioned but for the discussion created by the Labour representatives. All that had been said by the Presi- dent of the Local Government Board had been in reply to the criticisms of membors of the Labour party in the House.

Mr. OWENS thought that Mr. Crosfield's reply on the unemployed question was most inadequate. Mr. Burns was paying back part of the money left from the grant of £200,000 into the treasury and refusing to grant it to West Ham and other places which were badly affected. The matter then dropped. THE FUTURE OF LABOUR. With regard to the proposed unification of the local Labour forces the Council recently put a question to the affiliated branches as to whether they were in favour of the rules of the Council being so altered as to enable it to take political action. The Secretary said replies in the affirmative had been received from the Wheel- wrights and Smiths, Postmen, Tramway Workers, Car- penters and Joiners, Glass Makers, Cabinet Makers, Amalgamated Society of Railway Servants, Wire- drawers, Hairdressers, Insurance Agents, Bakers, Braziers, and Metal Works. Only about half the affiliated societies had replied. It was decided that the delegates should bring the matter before their branches with the object of obtain- ing a definite reply before the next Council meeting. THE WORK OF THE EDUCATION COMMITTEE

QUESTION OF SCHOOL FEES Mr. E. PLINSTON then presented an interesting report on the proceedings of the Education Committee during the past twelve months. He said a great amount of quiet work had been done to keep in touch with educa- tional affairs. During the year he had attended 29 meet- , ings out of a possible 29 and 11 rota meetings, the latter being the highest attendances put in by any member of the authority. One important question which had been I brought up was the remuneration of bricksetters' ! labourers engaged in the erection of the Beamont school. ;The matter would probably be raised again in connec- tion with the Bolton school, and he should fight for the I just rights of that class of workers. With regard to the ' refusal of the Board of Education to allow school fees Ito be charged at the Beamont school, the question of , freeing the whole of the schools was fought out, but. ! Mr. Jolley's motion in favour of that policy was I defeated. Therefore at the present time fees were being I charged in some schools whilst others were free. He ! believed the authority would be wise in deciding to abolish school foes and he would support that course whenever the matter came up again. There was a great I deal of discussion at the instance of the National Union I of Teachers, with reference to the appointment of head- master of the Beamont schoel. While not entirely in 1 agreement with creating appointments for local men ' only, he was in favour of a preference being given to Warrington teacher's. lie believed there were teachers in Warrington who applied for the post and who were quite as qualified for it as the successful candidate. All conditions being equal with regard to ability, he would always support the giving of preference to local men. The percentage of attendances at the schools of \Var. rington was at present lower than it had been for years owing to the great amount of sickness that was rampant. The examination conducted by the late Dr. Gornall showed a great many children to be suffering from defects in eyesight and hearing and from mental and physical ailments. He believed the environment of many children was responsible for their physical condition. He was not suggesting that all responsibilty should be taken away from the parents but when they found that in numerous cases, through no fault of the parents, children were suffering and therefore unable to attend schdol properly, it was the duty of the State to take action. In Warrington the total number of children reported by the teachers as being defective was 1390 and of these only 65 were ; found by the doctor to have no defects. Where parental ; neglect was the cause, the parents ought to be dealt 4 with—.(hear, hear)—but in many cases the authority should do something to place the children in better conditions. The recently-opened Beamont school was one of the finest for many miles around Warrington and he believed the Bolton school would be quite equal to ! it. As to the financial side of the committee's work, they had been told many times that Warrington did not get value for the money expended and that the com- mittee was extravagant. Official returns, however, ! showed that Warrington whilst being one of the ! lowest in regard to expenditnre was one of the ; highest towns in point of results. Mr. Plinston ; went into the question at some length and 1 continuing said the progress made during the I past four years had been eminently satisfactory. Many things, however, reqeired attention. He thought it was unfair to the working classes that the fees at the , Secondary school should be payable quarterly or per term instead of weekly. When the two Contemplated new schools were provided, Warrington would be well equipped educationally as compared with other towns, I and vast as the expenditure might appear, be thought ! they would benefit proportionately. While they desired as many children as possible to attend school beyond the age of thirteen, they thought that where the children were required to work to augment the miser- able wages of the parents, exemptions should be granted. Some discussion followed. Mr. RA.NDLW asked Mr. Plinston to urge forward the

Mr. GREENOUGH and Mr. OvvENs alleged that work had been done at the Beamont school by firms not pay- ing the standard rate of wages.

EMPIRE DAY.

Mr. OWENS asked whether the Education Committee had considered the celebration of Empire Day and the teaching of rifle shooting in the schools. Mr. PLENSTON said the question of celebrating Empire Day was raised and he thought the sub-com- mittee appointed to consider it were very wise in passing the matter over in a quiet manner. He believed the celebrations would be more fitting on the Ist of April. (Laughter.) Mr. OWENS said he disagreed with Mr. Plinston. , Mr. PLINSTON : It is only another method of teach- dug a Jingo policy and instilling into the minds of the :children the false policy of looking upon this vast Empire as something of which we can be absolutely proud. To my mind we cannot be proud of the Empire. We may boast of its greatness and magnitude but if we consider many of the things going on day by day we have to condemn them. lam not in agreement with Empire Day being, as it were, a day when teachers should instil into the minds of the children to look upon the magnitude of the Empire and to thank God that they live absolutely in England. If they would go intp the question and teach them the difficulties, and point out the troubles and assist in alleviating them, a celebration of a day of that kind would be very beneficial. With regard to rifle shooting, Mr. Plinston said he had not heard of the question being raised. With regard to fair wages, he had watched the matter as keenly as possible and the stereotyped reply that firms were pay- paying fair wages was always given. The fair wage clause in Warrington was a total failure. Councillor STOTT, in moving a vote of thanks to Mr Plinston, expressed regret that influence had been at work to prevent parents sending their children to the Beamont school. A statement was set abroad that only the scum of the town would attend it. With regard to the appointment of headmaster, he agreed with the action of the managers in appointing the candidate they considered to be best qualified, as there was no stipula- tion in the advertisement in favour of local men. The Trades Council had always supported reforms advoca- ted by the N.U.T., but he did not think the teachers treated the Trades Council fairly. If they appreciated what the Council did they would make haste to join hands with their fellow workingmen and become afalia- ted to the Council.

Mr. NTOORFIELD seconded and the vote was heartily accorded.

FAIR WAGES QUESTION.

Discussion took place with regard to the recent letting of a contract to the St. Helens Cable Company by the Electricity and Tramways Committee. On the proposition of Councillor Srorr, who again went fully into the question, a resolution was passed strongly protesting against the action of the Committee in giving out the work to the Company without havin,, proof given to them that the firm was adhering to the Standing Orders.

Mr. RITHBONE pointed out that the Wholesale Society was depreciating to such an extent that the whole of their indebtedness would be wiped off in 13 years. In Warrington it would take 20 years to achieve that object. Did they think it wise to spend their money instead of acting like the Wholesale and other Co-operative Societies ? If they did that where was their "cake ?" (Mr. Bouchier :We have it.) They had no more than other Societies and if they acted differ- ently from the other Societies they would not not be showing that progress which they ought to show. They could not always depend upon the same scource of profit and if they could not what would happen in the future if there was a run on the Society? One 31sainER, ventured to suggest that recent actions were an insult to Ar. Ra,thbone, but was unable to pro- ceed farther on account of the opposition with which the remark was greeted.

Unless the Society had assets which would enable them to continu) the business after paying off their debts they would have to go into liquidation, and that was a contingency they, had to guard against. (Hear, hear.) A MEMBEa at the back of the room seriously coin- plained that in considering the whole of the assets of the Society, the Special Committee had exceeded its duty. They only had to consider the property of the Society. Deprecia- tion is all right but it is not necessary to depreciate to that extent."
