He defied the auditor of the Society to take a single item in the ledger and say what it represented. They need not trouble themselves in the slightest bit about what the Wholesale Society was doing. They must look at their own Society and consider the position they were in. They owed in total 5:75,000 and when the Committee had finished their work it would be proved to them that against that they had assets which would retlize at least £ [30,000. With that enor- mous difference in their favour it was not necessary for the Society to continue to depreciate as they had done in their infancy. With half the total population of Warrington connected with the Society, and doing an absolutely cash trade, th‘y could not help but progress. The depreciation had gone as far as it had any need to go. Did they wish that their property should be written down until buildings which represented a value of many thousands of pounds stood in their books at nothing? That appeared to him to be the system upon which they had been working. Turning to a member who had earlier stated that certain people who had only been members of the Society for two years wanted to " boss the roost," Mr. Bouchier said he had been a member of the Society over 25 years.

WARRINGTON SWIMMING CLUB. Annual Dinner.

A very enjoyable function took place at Atkinson's Cafe, Bridge-street, on Thursday evening when the annual dinner in connection with the Warrington Swimming Club was held. In the earlier years of the Club this event recurred with great regularity but latterly it had been allowed to die out. A re-awakening of interest in the Club has however, resulted in the re-institution of the annual convivial gathering, and whilst Thursday's proceedings were largely in the nature of an experiment,so sucotssful were they that there is no doubt that that will be repeated in future years. There was a large attendance and an excellent repast was served by Messrs. Atkinson's,Ltd.,af ter which the evening was spent in speechmaking and song, the latter largely predominating. In happy speeches, Mr. E. DRAPER, who presided, submitted the usual loyal toasts, which were received with musical honours. Mr. C. H. Wool) was entrusted with the toast of the evening "The Warrington Swimming Club." As one of the oldest members of the Club he had, he said, seen it in various stages of its existence. It had passed through vicissitudes during his connection with it, but at no period of its existence had it been so vigorous as at the present time. (Hear, hear.) He considered that a healthy sign. (Hear, hear.) One thing which gave him gratification was to see that the sons of the men who took part in the Club's first galas were amongst the leading swimmers in the town. In the old days they had only a small bath bat they were very enthusiastic,and the best thing then was that the majority seemed bent upon teaching others the art and seemed never so happy as effhen introducing new corners to the baths. He thought that was what swimming clubs were really founded for and it was incumbent upon everyody, so far as they were able to help others and to get them to join the Club. They were a very vigorous organization and whilst they had not attained a very great age they were sound financially possessed an excellent set of officials and there was no reason why they should not go on prospering for a long time to come. (Applause.) They must never omit to realize the fact that they had very great concessions from the Town Hall Parks and Baths Committee—con- cessions which were not granted in many towns—and these had helped them very materially, and he thought that with all their advantages it would be a very great discredit if they did not made a first class Club. (Applause.) Replying to the toast Mr. G. N. VERNON (hon. sec.) said the club had in the past been most successful and he hoped they would continue to be so. The work of the polo team last year was not bad but he hoped that this year they would do even better. They had got some new blood and with a little practice he thought they could aspire to premier honours in the Liverpool and District League. (Hear, hear). The Club, he thought deserved a great deal of praise for making swimming popular. In particular the schoolboys had been greatly helped by the provision of the various trophies, and he hoped the members would do their utmost to render their organizations even more popular. (Applause). The health of the artistes was drunk on the proposi- tion of Mr. Harding, and the toast of " the Chairman" given by Mr. F. Garside, was most enthusiastically received.

During the evening songs were rendered by Mr. J. H. Draper, Mr. E. Morris and Mr. J. Emson, concertina solos by Mr. R. Bloomfield, violin solos by Mr. A. Hitchen, and humorous recitations by H. M. Ashton. Mr. A. Brayshaw acted as accompanist. All the artistes were in excellent form and encores were the order of the evening.

A disastrous fire broke out in the premises of the Canada Laundry Company at Montreal on Thursday. Two women were killed and six seriously injured by leaping from the windows, all the exits from the build- ing having been cut off by the flames. A fine Dutch salmon was on view at a shop in Bond- street, London, on Thursday. It weighed 511b5., and measured in length 521 n., girth 28ia.

Mr. Seed opened the debate by moving: "That this meeting expresses its unabated confidence in the manager, desires to place on record its appreciation of the valuable services rendered by him during the 10 years he has been connected with the Society, recognizes in him and not the present Com- mittee the person who has placed the Society in its present sound financial position and trusts that he will continue in his present position to help to guide us for some time to come." Throughout the whole of England there was not a single Co-operative Society in a sounder financial position than the Warrington Society (" Question.") "Is it fair " continued Mr. Seed "that a man who has served the Society so well should find himself in this position? I don't think so." (Elear,

M. J. CoTTErtru. seconded. The manager had not been treated with the courtesy he was entitled to by some members of the committee who had been recently elected and, honestly speaking, he had been compelled to resign. Mr. OxTos moved that the meeting support the action of the committee. If they had not got confidence in what the committee did they should call upon them to resign. Mr. W. Mow, who prefaced his remarks by the state- ment that he had been a member of the Society for 35 years, seconded. No one appreciated Mr. itathbone's work more than he did, and he was speiking without wishing to give offence, more especially to Mr. Rath- bone. The men who formed the Society 40 years ago were self-sacrificing man, and men of ability, but the ideas of 40 years ago would not do for to-day. If wound up at once the Society could pay 30s. in the OC, and when they were in that position it was not neces- sary that they should continue to add to the reserve fund year by year as they were doing, especially as the reserve fund came out of their pocket.s. It belonged to the members and should be given to them in the way of dividend. They had a building fund of £4,000, and they had recently built a shop at Latchford. Where did that money come from? Out of the building fund ? It did not. It came out of the profits which should have been returned to the members. They were making an addition to the Winwick-street property, and that money was coming out of the profits instead of the building fund. _

Mr. kA.THBONE said that during the time he had been manager of the Society hehad put the undertaking in a position which he challenged any other Society in the country to better. If they wanted to keep that position they must not pay their income away as they got it. If it had not been for the early savings they would not at the present time have been able to pay 2s. in the £ dividend.

At this stage Mr. MA.LoNE interjected a remark which caused the manager to retort "I am sorry to say that you are not one of the best men on the Committee." . WARRINGTON CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY.

LIVELY SCENES AT THE QUARTERLY

MEETING.

The Manager's Position.

RESIGNATION HANDED IN TO COMMITrEE.

There were lively scenes at the quarterly meeting of the Warrington Co-operative Society on Tuesday even- ing. Mr. T. Critchley took the chair at eight o'clock and it was three hours later that the proc.3edings were closed by a motion to adjourn the question at that time under discussion.

THE BALANCE SHEET. The balance sheet which was approved showed that the sales recorded were the largest in the history of the Society.

===== 0002601_19070420_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MANAGER'S POSITION.

The next business was the reading of the minutes of the Committee meetings. At one meeting the entry read:

Finding it difficult to give satisfaction, as complaints were being constantly made with an apparent desire to depreoate him, the manager (Mr. Rathbone) asked the committee to listen to a statement in which he desired to point out certain conditions appertaining to his appointment. He also pointed out what was the original intention of the founders of the Society with respect to depreciation. He also said he could no longer remain in such an unsatisfactory position as to be measured by a man with less than a month's experi- ence against the 43 years of the fullest devotion on his (the Manager's) part, and therefore, as a protest he was reluctantly compelled to ask the Committee to accept his resignation. In a report which he presented to the committee Mr. Rathbone said that as at the annual meeting a member made it appear as though the other members were not having justice fully dealt out to them by reason of the depreciation. He begged to point out that the practice of depreciation commenced in IS(4 when the Society was in low , water and had continued up to the present time. "Then surely," said 1 Mr. Rathbone "when the Society is capable of paying 3s. in the .£ it is not desirable to take a retrograde movement such as was submitted at the general meeting, by withholding the depreciation, as it can only be looked upon as a selfish, unprecedented 1 course of action, without the slightest justification and which would bring disgrace upon the Society through- out the whole of the co-operative movement. The Society is asked to discontinue the depreoiation so that larger dividends may be paid and to ignore the desire of the founders to provide for the future by putting the Society on such a firm foundation as to defy any boycott which might take place. Asa protest I therefore give notice of my intention to retire from the position of manager of this. important Society,and to leave it in as sound a financial position as any in the whole movement."

At a special meeting of the Committee which was subsequently held it was resolved " That it is with regret that we receive and accept Mr. Rathbone's resignation because of his long and faithful services to the Society, coupled with his declining years—accord- ing to the report read at the meeting of the Committee on February 13th we can do no other than accept his decision to resign—and that he be asked to fix a date for the termination of his services." At a further meeting it was resolved : "We as a Board request the present manager to state a definite time when he will retire," and Messrs. Farrar and Hlbbert were appointed a deputation to wait upon the manager in this connection.

At he outset it was decided to limit each speaker to three minutes.

Might they hold fast the sure and certain hope of the resurrection, the promise of a life wherein they would meet once more those" they had loved long since and lost awhile:"

...mtmm0tff..101...N...• HOVIS.

What It Is. INTERESTING PACTS.

Large numbers of• those who visited the Trades Exhibition re3ently held in the Parr Hall,were naturally very much interested in the Hovis flour and bread department. No doubt many of these, as well as the general public, would like to know some of the facts connected with the growth of the Hovis industry, and what Hovis flour and bread really are. What is the signification of the word " Hovis ?" It is a patent composite word invented by a young Cambridge under- graduate.lt is made up of the two Latin words,"Flofninis Vis "—the Strength of Man. And" Strength of Man" well describes this bread, which contains within its dainty loaves the very essence of the good golden wheat which is the life-support of the world. Originally Hovis was manufactured at Macclesfield. Its head- quarters are still there, but the Hovis Company having outgrown its acco=modation has now also manufac- tories in London, Manchester, and South Africa. _

It may not be ;tmiss to state that the -;wheat grain itself cannot be further improved upon. The grain from which the cakes in the days of Pharoah were made is the same as the wheat of to-day. The different stages in the history of milling are plainly marked after the first days of rather hazy conjecture. The first method that is known was a simple pounding of the wheat between two stone slabs. The next in the evolution was the introdnction of a simple mill in which the bottom stone was stationary and the upper one revolved by hand, grinding the corn between the two and allowing the particles to drop through a tiny hole in the lower stone. The present style of milling is simply the result of improvement in method and not in principle. Power, in the form of water-wheels and wind-mills,reduced the labour and increased the output, steam and electricity supplied more power, and a still greater output, and eventually the steel roller displaced the stone, until improvement came to a standstill. The three parts which, broadly speaking, make up each grain are the bran or husk, the white starchy part from which white flour is made, and the germ. Science was able to demonstrate that the bran is a negligible quantity from a food standpoint ; it is an irritant and indigestible. The white flour is good as a food, and the germ exception- ally so. The white flour is comparatively easy to manipulate, but the germ is not so tractable. When passed through the rollers it is turned into flakes and does not grind into powder as the white flour does. It is soft and has certain properties which white flour does not possess. It is extremely rich—far richer than white flour—in phosphates, nitrogenous matter and album!. noids, or, in other words, the natural building materials for renewing mental and bodily strength, for enriching the blood, and generally keeping the body well fed with most easily digestible material. This is where Flovis comes in. _ R A MILLE SCIENTIST.

Some eighteen years ago, :qr. Richard Smith, a miller and a scientist, went to these Macclesfield millers with a discovery which he had recently made,and the discovery was in this wise. He had ascertained by long-tried experiments that the essence and life of wheat lay in the germ. This germ he carefully extracted, specially prepared it, added some salt, and mixed it in good proportions with the best white flour, thus producing what was then known as germ flour, and what was afterwards changed to its present name of Hovis. In ordinary brown bread you only get 1 per cent, of this life-giving germ. It was originally waste product with the bran. Now it is extracted separately by a special process from wheat from all parts of the world. The Hovis Company people specially treat the germ by a cooking process, because only thus can it be properly preserved. If it were put raw into the flour it would turn the flour rancid, so much oil is there in this little germ. The germ contains the most nourishing qualities of the wheat—all those qualities which go to the formation of bone and muscle, and consequently Hovis bread is con- sidered by the doctors as the most nitrogenous bread in existence. Indeed it has been proved by analysis that one and a half pounds of Hovis bread contain more nourishment than one pound of ordinary white bread together with a rich red half-pound of beefsteak. The Hovis firm now turns out enough flour to make 50 million loaves of Hovis bread every year, and this, of course, exclusive of the immense amount of ordinary white flour which they disp ttch yearly from their mills. It is claimed that for bone forming, for muscle form- ing, for feeding the brain tissues, for building up con- stitutions, and keeping them at concert pitch, Hovis is the ideal food before the public to-day. " its food value," says the "Lancet," "both as regards nitrogen and phosphates, is, broadly speaking, double that of bread made from ordinary wheaten flour." Dr. Gordon Stables, in "Fresh air treatment of Consumption," says: "The bread I use is Hovis, I am enthusiastic on it." Other medioal authorities speak with equal favour of

THE RAILWAY INSTITUTE.

OPENING OF BOWLING GREEN AND

RIFLE RANGE. Visit of Mr. and Mrs. Frank Ree.

The step Which the London and North-Western Railway Company took twelve months ago in providing for the wholesome recreation of their Warrington employees by placing a Railway Institute at their dis- posal has been fully justified. The scheme was taken up with great enthusiasm and the work of the Institute so far has been conspicuously successful. Last even- ing saw the inauguration of further recreative facilities, Mr. Frank flee, the chief goods manager, Easton, and Mrs. flee visiting Warrington to open a bowling green and rifle range. The bowling green has been laid near Arpley Station and the transformation which the plot of land has undergone Iduring the past three months is most striking. -The members have taken a keen interest in the construc- tion of the green and, with the assistance given by the company, have been the means of arranging enjoy- ment for the summer months Which will no doubt be greatly appreciated. The green presents a pretty picture, for part of the land has been planted with shrubs, and a rockery improves the effect. There is 3 well-appointed pavilion and the whole of the ground is enclosed. The railway men already have a strong bowling team which in 1903 bad the satisfaction of winning the cup offered in the Warrington Works Competition. The rifle range adjoins the Institute at Bank Quay, and is admirably fitted up. It is composed of three carriages laid continuously and givinga range in accord- ance with the Miniature Rifle Association standard of 25 yards. There are two targets, and the end and sides of the range are protected .by steel plates. The interior is painted pale green, and is fitted throughout with incandescent light. A special apparatus winds the target to and from the firing point, and this prevents risk of anyone having to wale between the firing point and the target and dispenses with a marker, as the appli- ance will admit of the target being brought back for examination of the result of the firing. The rifles have been presented by Mr. Frank Ree and are of the War Office miniature type. The members of the Institute .have affiliated themselves with the Miniature Rifle Association and thereby qualified to take part in com- petitions promoted by the Association in every part of the country. They are also eligible to compete for the Queen's Cup. Mr. E. ROBINSON, the secretary of the Institute. has been indefatigable in promoting its success and in arousing interest in the rifle range project, whilst Mr. T. F. Whittle has devoted a great deal of time to the arrangements for the bowling green. OPENING CEREMONIES.

- - --- Considerable interest was taken in last evening's opening proceedings, Mr. and Mrs. Ree coming specially to Warrington for the occasion. Mrs. Rae has taken an active interest in social work amongst railway men and on the opening of the Institute was the first to send a supply of _useful books.

===== 0002601_19070420_art0190.txt | included=true | included =====
The 6owllng green was opened first by Mr. Ree who lost in a short game with Mr. F'. T. Kinsman, outdoor goods manager, Manchester. At the close Mr. T. Horrabin, captain of the London and North-Western Works Competition team, presented a set of bowls suitably inscribed to the chief goods manager, and Mr. A. E. Cresswell, locomotive department, Warrington, presented a set to the outdoor goods manager. The party then proceeded to the rifle range where, in the presence of a large gathering, Mrs. Ree fired the first shot and registered a" bull's eye." Afterwards a meeting was held in the Conservative Club, Assembly Room, Mr. L T. Williams, the popular district goods manager, presiding. The CHAIRMAN on behalf of the assembly extended to Mr. and Mrs. Ree a most cordial welcome to Warrington. (Applause.) In coming specially from London to take part in the proceedings they had given an immense amount of pleasure, not only to those who were present but to those who were on duty, to say nothing of the encouragement their presence gave to those who had been actively engaged in social work in this town. They might regard that day as a red letter day in the history of the institute. (Applause.) It was just ' twelve months since they opened the institute and their indefatigable secretary Mr. Robinson had prepared a statemAnt on the year's work. The finances were substantially sound--(applause) and the work had been in every way satisfactory. During the year there were played 4,424 games of billiards 1,925 games of bagatelle, and there had been 29,850 ;hots at the miniature shooting range. They had run two very successful concerts and at Christmas time there was a tournament with 240 competitors, 52 prizes being distributed. These facts more than justified the existence of the institute. (Applause.) The shoot- ing range which Mrs. Rea had fittingly inaugurated by registering a bull's eye would be a very valuable addition Re believed it was the first range of its kind on the ' North-Western system so that in that respect they were in the happy position of knowing that Warrington wa.4 leading the way—(applause)—and leading the way in a movement which was becoming most popular all over the country. He would shortly expect to see that Enston, Liverpool, Manchester, and Birmingham were following Warrington. (Laughter.) They were indebted absolutely to Mr. Ree for the shooting range —(applause)—and Mr. Kinsman had given substantial help in obtaininc, the bowling green which would fill a want that had been felt for some time. (Applause.) They bad now a fully equipped institute in almost every detail which would provide rational recreation for the men during the summer months as well as through the winter. Mr. Williams concluded by asking Mrs. Ree's acceptance of a souvenir of the occasion in the form of a beautiful silver bowl, suitably inscribed, and containing choice orchids.

Mrs. ttEE said: "I am not a suffragette and I have never made a speech. This is just a family party and I would like to say I have been delighted to be here." (Applause.)

, Mr. REE then addressed the gathering. When he inspected the institute, he said, he marvelled at the wonderful transformation. He remembered the grimy old offices and found them trans- formed into an institute while the one-time blacks desolate looking ground around it was now a garden. The rifle range would, he hoped, interest the men in the national movement promoted by Lord Roberts and almost equally by the Secretary of State for War. At the time of the South African war I,Varrington North-Western men did nobly. (Applause.) Three members of their continent_--Blackwood,a fore- man, Lloyd, a labourer, and Maddock, the head shunter —lost their lives, a:sd their names were inscribed on a tablet at the London offices of the company. A knowledge of rifle shooting would be useful in case of such a contingency arising again, and he believed there would be the same numbers or more from the 'North-Western of Warrington to volun- teer their services. (Applause.) The Committee of the Institute had done wonders, but he had not lived ten years in Lancashire without learning that Lanca- shire men were thorough whether it was at work or play. PENKETH.

CONGO PROTEST IfEETING.--A meeting to protest against the Congo atrocities was held in the Wesleyan Church on Wednesday evening. There was a good attendance, Mr. 0. E. Parker presiding. An address des- cribing the horrible state of affairs in the Congo was delivered by the Rev. Padfield, returned missionary,who was one of the passengers on the " Jebba."—On the motion of Mr. J. G. Withinshaw,seconded by Mr. Jenks a resolution of protest was passed.

CARGO FLEET IRON CO.

Some time ago the Cargo Fleet Iron Company, Middlesborough, secured two big contracts for steel rails from the Argentine railway. The first shipment in fulfilment of these contracts has how been made, and has been subjected to most exhaustive tests by Lloyd's shrveyors and other experts, with the result that the steel, which was made by the Talbot process installed in the Cargo Fleet Works, has been pronounced superior' to any other turned out in this country. And more than this, says the "Shipping Gazette," German' makers secured orders from the same railways at the same time and it is now officially declared that the German steel has failed to stand the testa, and that, in consequence, the order placed with the German makers has been cancelled. By the way, the South Durham Iron and Steel Company,whose shares are largely held by the Cargo Fleet Company, has just declared a dividend at the rate of is. per share—which is another welcome feature in the situation.

TO COMMENCE IN THE " EXAMINER " NEXT WEER. BREATHLESS EXCITEMENT! THRILLING SENSATION ! ! • THE EXPLOITS OF THE NE-EYED MAN. EIGHT NARRATIVES • BY EDWARD TOWLER In which are related the amazing experiences of a human fiend who for a brief period terrorised England. This audacious scoundrel defied the police, and the smartest detectives of England and America were unable to lay him by the heels. He robbed a big bank in broad daylight, captured a specie van on our greatest railway, and kidnapped a multi-millionaire. Priceless pictures and jewels worth a king's ransom were among the booty secured by the fearless gang of outlaws led by the One-Eyed Man. A veritable Napoleon of crime, this twentieth century terror undertook to steal the signal books from a British warship, and actually succeeded ! It is AN ASTOUNDING RECORD and will be eagerly read. In many respects the series is unique. The tales are told without auy attempt at melodramatic embellishment, in a plain, straightforward way which makes them intensely realistic. They grip as power- fully as a strong serial, and anyone who reads the first story will want to follow the adven- tures of the One-Eyed Man to the end. LOOK OUT FOR (inr 111 13 ONE EYED MAN." All the warnings of the millionaires will however, have but little effect, and will not lessen one jot the striving of ' those anxious to assume the burdens of which they complain. . Big-Game Sportswomen.—Lady Minto, and Lady Eileen Elliot, the wife and daughter of the Viceroy of India, are the latest recruits to the band of adventurous sportswomen who have distineuished themselves by shooting big game. There would appear to be no lack of sport in that country, although it is supposed to be over-run with sportsmen, for out of four tigers killed in one afternoon, Lady alinto and her daughter each accounted for one. Among the big-game sportswomen Lady Constance Stewart-Richardson, the sister of the Countess of Cromartie, is reported to have shot most lions, bears and tigers,which she has sought at one time or another in the jungles of Asia, Africa, and America. The Duchess of Newcastle has also been on similar expeditions, whilst Lady Delamere, and Lady Hindly both spent their honeymoons on big game shoots in East Africa. Nowadays it is by no means uncommon for women to accompany their husbands on such ex- peditions, for which of course special outfits are neces- sary. This is usually of a semi-masculine character, but is kept as light in weight as possible, so as to be easily carried. Making Ones Own Dress Materials.—Undertaking ones own spinning and weaving at home may be all very well as a recreation, but apart from this point of view they can hardly be recommended as profitable employ- ments. The home worker, as a rule finds it as much as she can do to make her own clothes, without making the materials also. Still, if one has plenty of time, we are told by Miss Constance Smedley that it is possible for the amateur to obtain practical and gratifying results with a small handloom, such as one sees in nearly all the cottage homes of Sweden. Such a loom will allow of the making of a dress piece of 2d yards, of a width of 25 inches, and it can be utilised for the making of towels, household linen, and furniture hang- ings. Some technical knowledge is no doubt required, and this can usually be obtained at the Technical and Art Schools, but it is said that some workers are able with little instruction to conquer all the difficulties of hand-lnom weaving. Going to Extremes.—Both tailors and dressmakers in Paris and Vienna have been running to extremes lately in the matter of seams. The former have been using the scissors in the most extravagant manner upon the building fabrics entrusted to their care, while the latter having been priding themselves upon building a coat

The bLiscess's gift consisted of a set of enamel waistcoat 'dons, fashioned by her own hands. Four young men entered the room of the cashier of St. Petersburg on Tuesday, and, threatening the cashier and his assistant with revolvers, they stole £2OO and decamped. Germany is to send a liner to Dover on May 26 to convey to the Fatherland a number of British editors and journalists. This invitation is in recognition of British hospitality to German editors.

ST. HELENS HOTEL RAID. Thomas Andrew Pruett was charged at St. Helens on Monday with stealing £2O worth of jewellery, &c., from the Saddle Hotel, Westfield-street, on Saturday night last. The Chief Constable said that at seven " o'clock on Saturday night the articles were seen safe. Later Mr. Berry discovered the fact that a robbery had been committed. and Impector Dunn and Detective Roe traced the theft to the prisoner, and effected his arrest while be bad most of the stolen property in his possession. Mr. Berry said that prisoner was in the house on Saturday morning, and treated some men and told them that be had come from Wales to take up a position under an uncle at one of the collieries. He ' inquired if he could get lodgings in the neighbourhood. At seven o'clock in the evening prisoner went to the house again, and was served by Richard Savage. Later Savage saw prisoner coming downstairs, when he dis- armed supicion by saying, "Oh, I seo there is no billiards to-night. Detective-inspector Dunn said he found prisoner at his wife's house in Rigby-street on Sunday !morning with the stolen watches, &c., in his pockets, and Detective Roe took him to the Town Hall where Pruett said. "I done it wilfully, and you have got all the stuff." Pruett had been living at Wigan for some time apart from his wife. He had a record of twelve convictions, three for similar offences. The bench sent him to goal for six months' hard labour. Two workmen while cleaning furnaces in the cellars of the gas retorts at Llanelly on Tuesday were found dead from suffocation by gas fumes. The Prime:Mini4ter announced on Tuesday evening, in reply to a question, that he is not yet in a position to state when the Licensing Bill will be introduced. '

(Applause.) Mr. PARR then presented a handsome writing table to Mr. and Mrs. Rose, which had been supplied by Messrs. Robert Garnett and Sons. Mr. ROSE replied on behalf of Mrs. Rose and himself, expressed their deep gratification at the expression of confidence,and reviewed in an interesting manner the period which had elapsed since they came to Warring- ton, remarking that only three of the then trustees were now living, and stated that the presentation which had been made to them, and the kind recognition of the trustees, would be an incentive and a stimulus to them to endeavour to perform their duty in the future as in the past. NATIONAL FREE FREE CHURCH COUNCIL AND WOMEN I Mrs. Rendel Harris, whose husband (Dr. J. Rendel Harris) is president of the National Council of the 1 Evangelical Free Churches, is holding a series of Con- ventions for Women under the auspices of Free Church Councils during Dr. Rendel Harris's year of office. The meetings will include a Social Reception, Morning Devotional Meetings and afternoon Conference. In the evening meetings will be held addressed by prominent Free Church Women Workers including :—Mrs. Percy Bunting, Mrs. Herbert Lewis, Mrs. George Morgan,Mrs. Philip Snowden,Mrs. Charlotte Benham and others. All applications for particulars should be made to the head- quarters of the National Free Church Council,Memorial Hall, London, E.C.
